    • sign of chronic raised ICP → with normal MRV and high opening pressure (> 25 cmH2O) with normal CSF = idiopathic intracranial hypertension
    Visual fields — same patient (case 3)
    Findings
    • enlarged blind spots (A–C) progressing to peripheral constriction and dense field loss (D–F)
    Diagnosis
    • papilledema-related visual field loss → vision-threatening IIH needs acetazolamide ± weight loss
    • and optic nerve sheath fenestration / CSF shunt if progressing
    ABG on face mask (FiO2 0.4) — 71M, dyspnea, limb weakness, diplopia (case 5)
    Findings
    • pH 7.32
    • PaO2 12.4 kPa (≈ 93 mmHg) on FiO2 0.4 (P/F ≈ 233)
    • PaCO2 5.9 kPa (≈ 44 mmHg)
    • HCO3 28 mmol/L (28 mEq/L)
    • lactate 1.2 mmol/L (≈ 11 mg/dL)
    • BE +4.9
    • K 4.6 mmol/L (4.6 mEq/L)
    Diagnosis
    • acidemia with a "normal" PaCO2 in a breathless patient and a raised bicarbonate = early / impending hypercapnic (type 2) respiratory failure from neuromuscular weakness → myasthenic crisis
    • check FVC / NIF, ICU, intubate early (FVC < 20 mL/kg)
    Fundoscopy — 30M, right eye decreased vision (case 10)
    Findings
    • both optic discs look normal (no swelling, no pallor)
    • exam sheet: right RAPD, reduced acuity, red desaturation, central scotoma
    Diagnosis
    • retrobulbar optic neuritis ("the patient sees nothing and the doctor sees nothing")
    Swinging flashlight test — 32F, right decreased vision (case 10)
    Findings
    • both pupils constrict when the light is on the normal eye
    • when the light swings to the affected eye, both pupils dilate
    Diagnosis
    • relative afferent pupillary defect (Marcus Gunn pupil) → optic nerve lesion on the affected side
    MRI brain, FLAIR — same presentation (case 10)
    Findings
    • multiple ovoid hyperintense white-matter lesions — periventricular, perpendicular to the ventricles on sagittal views (Dawson fingers), callosal and juxtacortical
    • CSF oligoclonal bands positive (not in serum)
    Diagnosis
    • multiple sclerosis (dissemination in space ± time) presenting with optic neuritis → IV methylprednisolone
    • then disease-modifying therapy
    CT angiography — 25F, left-sided weakness and facial asymmetry, NIHSS 10 (case 8)
    Findings
    • abrupt cut-off / non-opacification of the right middle cerebral artery at M1
    • other circle-of-Willis vessels opacify
    Diagnosis
    • acute ischemic stroke from right M1 large-vessel occlusion → IV thrombolysis if < 4.5 h + mechanical thrombectomy (up to 24 h with favorable imaging)
    • young-stroke workup

    Rheumatology

    How to use this

    Rheumatology stations are either a "systemic" history (fever, rash, weakness, Raynaud, cytopenia in a young woman) or a joint / spine exam (hand exam for RA, back exam for axial SpA, plus knee, shoulder and hip exams). The systemic stations all use the same CTD-screen history and head-to-toe exam with a urine dipstick, so learn that block once.

    Open every station
    • introduce yourself
    • take permission
    • vitals
    • BMI
    • always finish the exam with a urine dipstick and offer fundoscopy
    Stations covered
    • scleroderma renal crisis
    • SSc with ILD (Raynaud)
    • adult-onset Still disease
    • dermatomyositis
    • IgA vasculitis (HSP)
    • EGPA
    • active SLE with ITP
    • SLE with TTP and seizure
    • Sjögren with distal RTA
    • RA (history + hand exam, and hand exam ×3)
    • psoriatic arthritis
    • ankylosing spondylitis (history ×2, back exam ×3)
    • knee exam (acute monoarthritis / gout), shoulder exam, hip exam (extra checklists)
    1. Core rheumatology history (every case)
    Presenting complaint
    • onset
    • duration
    • course
    • pattern
    • severity and effect on daily activity
    • aggravating / relieving factors
    • previous similar episodes
    • response to analgesics
    Constitutional
    • fever (documented, pattern, response to antipyretics)
    • weight loss
    • night sweats
    • fatigue
    • anorexia
    CTD screen
    • joint pain / swelling, morning stiffness
    • malar / discoid rash, photosensitivity
    • hair loss
    • oral / genital ulcers
    • sicca (dry eyes / mouth)
    • Raynaud
    • skin tightness, dysphagia / heartburn
    • serositis (pleuritic chest pain)
    • muscle weakness
    • red painful eyes
    • thrombosis and miscarriages
    Systemic review
    • CNS (LOC, seizure, headache, psychosis, weakness, numbness, visual loss)
    • respiratory (SOB, cough, hemoptysis, sinusitis)
    • cardiac (chest pain, palpitations, edema)
    • GI (abdominal pain, N/V, bloody diarrhea, dysphagia, GERD)
    • renal (hematuria, frothy urine, reduced output, edema)
    • hematologic (bleeding, bruising, nodes)
    Infection mimics
    • sick contacts
    • recent URTI / gastroenteritis
    • raw milk (brucella)
    • TB contact
    • travel
    • sexual history
    • transfusion
    Past history
    • known autoimmune disease (SLE, RA, APS, Sjögren, psoriasis, IBD, AS) — when diagnosed, organs involved, flares, admissions
    • DM, HTN, CKD, thyroid
    Drugs
    • analgesics / NSAIDs
    • steroids
    • DMARDs / immunosuppressants and adherence
    • HCQ
    • aspirin / anticoagulants
    • statins
    • new drugs (drug-induced lupus / vasculitis)
    • allergies
    Obstetric
    • miscarriages
    • preeclampsia
    • preterm birth
    • neonatal lupus / congenital heart block in a child
    • contraception
    Family and social
    • autoimmune disease, psoriasis, IBD, AS
    • smoking, alcohol, occupation, socioeconomic state
    2. History add-ons by presentation
    A. Raynaud / skin tightness
    Raynaud
    • triphasic color change (white → blue → red)
    • cold / stress trigger
    • painful
    • unilateral vs bilateral
    • digital ulcers
    Secondary clues
    • skin tightness (hands only vs proximal / trunk)
    • dysphagia
    • heartburn
    • exertional dyspnea (ILD / PH)
    • rash
    • joints
    Other causes
    • vibrating tools
    • vasoconstrictors (beta blockers, ergots)
    • hypothyroidism
    • smoking
    • paraproteinemia
    Renal crisis red flags
    • new severe headache, blurred vision, seizures, reduced urine
    • recent steroids
    • diffuse disease
    B. Fever + joint pain
    Fever
    • documented
    • daily pattern (quotidian evening spikes = AOSD)
    • response to antipyretics
    • sick contacts
    • raw milk
    • URTI
    Joints
    • distribution
    • symmetry
    • small vs large
    • morning stiffness
    • swelling
    Rash
    • evanescent salmon-pink rash with fever (AOSD)
    • malar (SLE)
    • psoriasis
    Sore throat, nodes, abdominal pain
    • AOSD
    • lymphoma
    • viral infection
    C. Proximal weakness
    Weakness
    • proximal (combing hair, climbing stairs, rising from a chair) vs distal
    • painful vs painless
    • fatigability
    • diurnal variation
    • dysphagia
    • diplopia
    Rash
    • facial photosensitive rash
    • heliotrope
    • hands (Gottron, mechanic's hands)
    Lung
    • dyspnea
    • cough (ILD in antisynthetase syndrome)
    Mimics
    • hypothyroidism
    • Cushing (steroids)
    • statins
    • hypokalemia (episodic paralysis, diarrhea, diuretics)
    • myasthenia (fatigable, ptosis)
    • GBS (ascending)
    • malignancy (DM is paraneoplastic in older adults)
    D. Purpuric rash (vasculitis)
    Rash
    • onset
    • site (legs)
    • palpable
    • painful / itchy
    • ulcers
    • pigmentation
    Trigger
    • recent URTI (IgA vasculitis)
    • new drugs
    • hepatitis B / C
    Organs
    • abdominal pain, GI bleeding
    • hematuria, frothy urine
    • joint pain
    • asthma, sinusitis, nasal crusting, hemoptysis
    • foot drop / numbness (mononeuritis multiplex)
    • testicular pain
    E. Back pain
    Inflammatory pattern
    • age < 40
    • insidious
    • > 3 months
    • morning stiffness > 30 min
    • improves with activity not rest
    • night pain (second half)
    • alternating buttock pain
    • good NSAID response
    Mechanical / red flags
    • trauma
    • fever
    • weight loss
    • sphincter or leg weakness
    • TB contact
    • raw milk (brucella spondylitis)
    • cancer
    SpA features
    • dactylitis
    • enthesitis (heel)
    • peripheral arthritis
    • uveitis
    • psoriasis
    • IBD symptoms
    • recent GI / GU infection
    • urethritis
    • family history
    F. Known SLE with new problem
    Flare
    • fatigue
    • fever
    • rash
    • hair loss
    • ulcers
    • arthritis
    • serositis
    • edema
    • frothy urine
    Bleeding / cytopenia
    • epistaxis, gum bleeding, bruising, melena, hematuria
    • anticoagulants / aspirin
    Neuro
    • seizure (onset, type, tongue bite, incontinence, post-ictal)
    • headache
    • confusion
    • psychosis
    • visual loss
    Treatment
    • current drugs
    • adherence
    • recent changes
    3. Core systemic exam (every "medical" rheumatology station)
    General
    • look
    • vitals (BP crucial — renal crisis, nephritis)
    • temperature
    • BMI
    Head and neck
    • pallor
    • jaundice
    • oral ulcers
    • dry mouth / dental caries
    • parotids
    • eyes (redness, Schirmer)
    • cervical and axillary nodes
    • nasal / ear lesions
    Skin
    • describe rash (distribution, size, color, palpable or not, ulcers), hair, nails
    • malar rash, livedo reticularis, purpura, psoriasis
    Joints
    • signs of active arthritis (swelling, warmth, tenderness, deformity, limited movement)
    • hands, enthesitis, dactylitis
    Neuro
    • LOC
    • cranial nerves
    • tone
    • power
    • reflexes
    • sensation
    • meningeal signs
    • fundoscopy
    Chest
    • breathing pattern
    • basal crackles (ILD)
    • effusion
    • pleural rub
    CVS
    • murmurs, rub, JVP, HF signs, loud P2 (PH), edema
    • pulses in all limbs, radio-radial and radio-femoral delay
    Abdomen
    • tenderness
    • hepatosplenomegaly
    • ascites
    • PR exam (GI vasculitis)
    Renal
    • flank tenderness
    • uremic signs
    • fluid overload
    • urine dipstick
    4. Exam add-ons and dedicated exam stations
    Hand exam (RA station, 50–60 marks)
    Start
    • wash hands, introduce, explain, consent
    • expose hands, wrists and elbows
    • sit with hands on a pillow
    • ask about pain
    Look — general
    • walking aids
    • splints
    • other joints
    Look — dorsum
    • posture
    • ulnar deviation
    • MCP / wrist subluxation
    • swelling
    • skin (bruising, thinning from steroids, psoriatic plaques)
    • Z-thumb
    • swan neck
    • boutonnière
    • Heberden (DIP) / Bouchard (PIP) nodes
    • interossei wasting
    • scars
    • nails (pitting, onycholysis, splinter hemorrhages)
    Look — palms
    • claw hand
    • contracture
    • scars (carpal tunnel release)
    • palmar erythema
    • Dupuytren thickening
    • thenar / hypothenar wasting
    • Janeway / Osler lesions
    Feel — palms up
    • temperature
    • radial / ulnar pulses
    • thenar / hypothenar bulk
    • palmar fascia
    • median and ulnar sensation
    Feel — palms down
    • radial sensation (first web space), temperature over wrist, MCPs, elbows
    • MCP squeeze test
    • bimanual palpation of MCP, PIP, DIP, CMC, wrist, anatomical snuffbox
    • elbows for nodules / plaques
    Move
    • active then passive finger and wrist flexion / extension
    • normal side first if painful
    Motor
    • radial (finger and wrist extension)
    • ulnar (finger abduction)
    • median (thumb abduction)
    Function
    • power grip, pincer grip, pick up a coin
    • undo a button / write
    Special tests
    • Tinel and Phalen for carpal tunnel
    Close
    • thank and cover
    • offer neurovascular exam of the upper limbs, elbow and shoulder exam, and a full systemic exam
    Examiner Qs
    • DDx RA, PsA, SLE (Jaccoud), OA
    • investigations = hand X-ray (then US / MRI), RF, anti-CCP, ANA, ESR / CRP, aspiration if indicated
    • RA X-ray = soft tissue swelling, periarticular osteopenia, joint space narrowing, marginal erosions (late subluxation / ankylosis)
    Back / spine exam (AS station, 70 marks)
    Start
    • introduce
    • confirm name
    • explain
    • consent
    • wash hands
    • expose the upper body
    • stand the patient
    • ask about pain
    Inspection
    • general (habitus, aids, wasting)
    • anterior (scars, shoulder asymmetry, pelvic tilt)
    • lateral (cervical lordosis, thoracic kyphosis — question-mark posture, loss of lumbar lordosis)
    • posterior (scoliosis, wasting, scars)
    Gait
    • limping
    • Trendelenburg
    • waddling
    Palpation
    • spinous processes
    • interspinous ligaments
    • paraspinal muscles
    • SI joints
    Movement (active then passive)
    • cervical flexion, extension, lateral flexion, rotation
    • lumbar flexion, extension, lateral flexion, rotation
    Special tests
    • occiput-to-wall distance
    • chest expansion (< 2.5 cm abnormal)
    • modified Schober (< 5 cm increase abnormal)
    • straight leg raise
    • femoral stretch
    • SI joint tests (FABER, Gaenslen, compression)
    Complete with
    • other joints
    • enthesitis (Achilles)
    • dactylitis
    • nails and skin (psoriasis, keratoderma blennorrhagicum, circinate balanitis)
    • eyes (uveitis)
    • oral ulcers
    • lung apices (apical fibrosis)
    • aortic regurgitation murmur
    • neuro exam of the legs
    Examiner Qs
    • DDx AS, PsA, reactive arthritis, IBD arthropathy, brucellosis, TB
    • X-ray changes in AS = SI erosions / sclerosis → ankylosis, squaring of vertebrae, shiny corners, syndesmophytes, ligament calcification → bamboo spine
    Knee exam (station: 45M, right knee pain and limited movement for 2 weeks)
    Start
    • wash hands, introduce, confirm name and DOB, explain, consent
    • expose both lower limbs
    • stand the patient first
    • ask about pain
    Look — general
    • gait (antalgic)
    • walking aids
    • other joints
    • psoriasis
    Look — knee
    • anterior (scars, bruising, swelling, patella position, valgus / varus)
    • lateral (fixed flexion, hyperextension)
    • posterior (scars, popliteal swelling — Baker cyst)
    • quadriceps wasting
    Feel
    • temperature (compare sides)
    • quadriceps bulk
    • joint lines, patella, quadriceps tendon, tibial tuberosity, fibular head
    • popliteal fossa (cyst, aneurysm)
    Effusion tests
    • sweep / bulge test (small)
    • patellar tap (moderate–large)
    • ballottement
    Move
    • active then passive flexion and extension, normal side first
    • hyperextension
    Special tests
    • anterior drawer and Lachman (ACL)
    • posterior drawer / sag (PCL)
    • valgus stress (MCL)
    • varus stress (LCL)
    • McMurray (menisci)
    Close
    • screen other joints
    • examine hip and ankle
    • neurovascular exam of the leg
    • thank and cover
    DDx (acute monoarthritis)
    • septic arthritis
    • gout
    • pseudogout (CPPD)
    • reactive arthritis
    • hemarthrosis
    • systemic arthritis (RA, SLE, SpA)
    • trauma (ligament / meniscus)
    Workup
    • joint aspiration (cell count and differential, Gram stain and culture, polarized microscopy for crystals)
    • CBC
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • coagulation (PT / aPTT / INR) if hemarthrosis
    • uric acid
    • immunology (ANA, RF, anti-CCP, anti-dsDNA)
    • imaging (X-ray both knees)
    Acute gout treatment
    • counsel
    • rest the joint, ice
    • NSAID if no contraindication or colchicine
    • otherwise steroid (intra-articular, IM or oral)
    • IL-1 inhibitor (anakinra / canakinumab) if all contraindicated
    • low-purine diet, less alcohol and fructose
    • continue allopurinol if already on it
    • start urate-lowering later if recurrent attacks, tophi, CKD or stones (target urate < 6 mg/dL = 360 µmol/L)
    Shoulder exam (station: 60M, right shoulder pain and limited movement for 2 years)
    Start (WIPER)
    • wash hands, introduce, confirm details, permission, position, pain, expose the upper body, stand on the patient's right
    • examine joints above and below
    • normal side first
    Inspect (front, side, back)
    • posture and symmetry
    • arm position (abducted + externally rotated = anterior dislocation; adducted + internally rotated = posterior)
    • SC joint, clavicle, AC joint
    • skin (scars, bruising, swelling, sinuses)
    • wasting (deltoid — squared shoulder, supraspinatus, infraspinatus, pectorals)
    • winged scapula (push against wall — long thoracic nerve)
    • axilla (nodes, effusion, bruising)
    Palpate (skin → muscle / tendon → bone)
    • temperature, swelling
    • SC joint → clavicle → AC joint → greater and lesser tuberosities → glenohumeral joint → spine of scapula → coracoid
    • muscle bulk and crepitus
    • biceps tendon in the bicipital groove ("Popeye" sign = long-head rupture)
    • supraspinatus insertion
    • watch the face
    Move (active, demonstrate first)
    • flexion 180°
    • extension 65°
    • abduction 180°
    • adduction 50°
    • external rotation 80° (elbows at sides, flexed 90° — lost early in adhesive capsulitis, < 30°)
    • internal rotation (hand behind back to scapula, T6–T7 — Apley scratch)
    • scapular movement
    • then passive in all directions feeling for crepitus
    Rotator cuff tests
    • empty can / Jobe (supraspinatus)
    • painful arc 60–120° (supraspinatus impingement)
    • external rotation against resistance (infraspinatus, teres minor)
    • external rotation in abduction (teres minor)
    • Gerber lift-off (subscapularis)
    Impingement
    • Neer (passive full flexion with internal rotation)
    • Hawkins-Kennedy (90° flexion + passive internal rotation)
    AC joint
    • scarf test (cross-body adduction → pain / crepitus at AC joint)
    Instability
    • apprehension test (abduction + external rotation) → relocation test → release
    • sulcus sign (inferior)
    • anterior / posterior drawer of the humeral head
    Biceps
    • Speed test (resisted forward flexion with elbow extended and supinated)
    • Yergason test (resisted supination with elbow flexed 90°)
    Close
    • examine cervical spine and elbow
    • distal neurovascular exam
    • thank and cover
    • present findings
    DDx
    • rotator cuff disorder (tendinitis, tear)
    • glenohumeral disease (OA, crystals, RA, SpA, adhesive capsulitis)
    • AC joint disease
    • referred pain (cervical spine, cardiac, diaphragm)
    • trauma, infection (rare)
    Workup
    • imaging (shoulder X-ray — degeneration, crystals, erosions; ultrasound then MRI)
    • US-guided aspiration (cells, crystals, culture) if effusion
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • immunology (RF, ACPA, HLA-B27)
    • CBC, renal, liver profile
    Management (degenerative)
    • education
    • relative rest
    • NSAIDs if safe
    • physiotherapy (range of motion, strengthening, ice)
    • intra-articular / subacromial steroid injection
    • orthopedics for arthroplasty or cuff repair
    Hip exam (station: 55M, hip pain for 2 months)
    Start
    • wash hands, introduce, confirm details, explain, consent
    • expose lower limbs
    • stand first
    • ask about pain
    Look
    • general inspection
    • lower limbs from front, side and back standing (scars, wasting, pelvic tilt, lumbar lordosis, leg rotation / shortening)
    • gait (antalgic, Trendelenburg)
    • repeat inspection lying
    Feel
    • hip temperature
    • greater trochanter tenderness (trochanteric bursitis)
    • apparent leg length (umbilicus → medial malleolus) and true leg length (ASIS → medial malleolus)
    Move
    • active flexion / extension
    • passive flexion (normal ≈ 120°)
    • internal and external rotation with hip and knee at 90° (internal rotation lost first in OA)
    • abduction and adduction (stabilize the pelvis)
    • extension lying prone
    Special tests
    • Thomas test (fixed flexion deformity — flex the opposite hip fully, the tested thigh lifts off the bed)
    • Trendelenburg test (stand on the affected leg — pelvis drops on the opposite side = weak gluteus medius / hip pathology)
    Close
    • neurovascular exam of both legs
    • examine the lumbar spine and knee
    • imaging
    DDx
    • hip OA
    • inflammatory arthritis (RA, AS)
    • avascular necrosis (steroids, alcohol, sickle cell)
    • trochanteric bursitis
    • septic arthritis
    • fracture
    • referred pain from lumbar spine (radiculopathy) or SI joint
    • meralgia paresthetica
    • hernia
    Other exam add-ons
    Scleroderma
    • skin tightness and its extent (beyond MCPs / elbows / knees = diffuse), sclerodactyly, digital ulcers / pitting scars, calcinosis, telangiectasia, microstomia, nailfold capillaroscopy, tendon friction rub
    • basal crackles
    • loud P2
    • BP and fundus
    Dermatomyositis
    • heliotrope rash, Gottron papules / sign, shawl and V signs, mechanic's hands
    • proximal power, tone, reflexes (normal), no fasciculation
    • swallow
    • crackles
    Sjögren
    • dry mouth
    • caries
    • parotid enlargement
    • Schirmer test
    • nodes / spleen (lymphoma)
    Vasculitis
    • palpable purpura
    • livedo
    • ulcers
    • nasal crusting / saddle nose
    • wheeze
    • foot drop / sensory loss
    • BP
    • dipstick
    5. Case scenarios

    Each case: stem → key positives → workup and results → diagnosis → management → examiner's questions.

    Scleroderma
    1. Scleroderma renal crisis (35F, diffuse SSc for 1 year, sudden headache)
    Clues
    • diffuse SSc (face, arms, chest), Raynaud, GERD
    • on aspirin, nifedipine, esomeprazole
    • first severe throbbing headache with blurred vision
    • BP 210/130, HR 110
    • sclerodactyly, telangiectasia, tendon friction rub
    • flame hemorrhages on fundoscopy
    • dipstick protein +++, blood +++
    DDx
    • diffuse SSc with renal crisis
    • TTP
    • HUS
    • other MAHA
    Workup
    • CBC + peripheral smear
    • hemolysis panel (LDH, haptoglobin, bilirubin, retics)
    • renal profile (creatinine, eGFR, BUN)
    • liver profile
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • urine (urinalysis with microscopy, 24-h protein or UPCR)
    • immunology (ANA, anti-dsDNA, Scl-70, anticentromere, anti-RNA polymerase III, anti-U1 RNP, aPL, C3 / C4)
    • mimics (glucose, HbA1c, TSH, FT4, HBV, HCV, HIV)
    • imaging (CXR, HRCT, echo, CT brain + MRA / MRV, renal Doppler US)
    • fundoscopy
    Results
    • BP 210/130 mmHg
    • WBC 2.9 ×10⁹/L
    • Hb 12 g/dL (120 g/L)
    • platelets 90 ×10⁹/L
    • schistocytes
    • creatinine 350 µmol/L (≈ 4.0 mg/dL)
    • urea normal
    • ESR 60 mm/h
    • CRP 50 mg/L
    • ANA 1:320 nucleolar
    • Scl-70 and anti-RNA polymerase III positive
    • anti-dsDNA, anticentromere, aPL negative
    • C3 / C4 normal
    • 24-h protein 1 g
    • urine 15 RBC/hpf
    • imaging, glucose, TSH, viral serology normal
    • fundus flame hemorrhages
    Diagnosis
    • diffuse systemic sclerosis with scleroderma renal crisis (with MAHA)
    Management
    • admit
    • rheumatology, nephrology and hematology consults
    • captopril 6.25 mg, then 12.5 mg, titrate up to 300 mg/day
    • target SBP fall of 20 mmHg in 24 h
    • follow Hb, platelets, creatinine, LDH, proteinuria
    • add CCB (amlodipine) or bosentan if BP still high
    • dialysis if needed
    • ACEi continued even on dialysis
    • avoid high-dose steroids
    2. Systemic sclerosis with ILD (30F, painful finger color change with cold for 6 months)
    History
    • triphasic color change
    • fingertip ulcer
    • skin tightness of hands / face
    • rash
    • joints
    • oral ulcers
    • SOB
    • chest pain
    • heartburn / regurgitation
    • uni- vs bilateral
    • vibrating tools
    • vasoconstrictor drugs
    • hypothyroidism
    • known rheumatic disease
    • smoking
    Clues
    • bilateral asymmetric painful attacks
    • one fingertip ulcer
    • tight skin of the dorsum of hands
    • exertional dyspnea
    Exam
    • vitals
    • skin tightness beyond the MCPs and its extent
    • arthritis
    • face (tightness, telangiectasia, small mouth)
    • hands (ulcers, sclerodactyly, capillaroscopy)
    • lung bases
    • loud P2
    Findings
    • tightness extending to trunk and legs
    • sclerodactyly
    • bibasal crackles
    DDx
    • systemic sclerosis
    • SLE
    • vasculitis
    • paraproteinemia
    Workup
    • CBC
    • liver profile
    • renal profile + urinalysis + BP (renal crisis)
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • immunology (ANA, anti-topoisomerase / Scl-70, anticentromere, anti-RNA polymerase III, anti-Jo-1, anti-RNP)
    • cardiopulmonary (echo for PASP, PFT with DLCO, 6MWT, NT-proBNP)
    • imaging (CXR, HRCT)
    • nailfold capillaroscopy
    Results
    • anemia, ANA positive, anti-topoisomerase positive, ILD on CT
    • PFT restrictive
    Diagnosis
    • diffuse systemic sclerosis with interstitial lung disease
    Renal crisis risk factors
    • diffuse skin disease (early, rapidly progressive)
    • glucocorticoids (prednisone > 15 mg)
    • anti-RNA polymerase III
    • cyclosporine
    • large-joint contractures
    • new anemia
    • new cardiac events (HF, pericardial effusion)
    Management
    • avoid cold, keep hands warm, stop smoking
    • anti-reflux measures (small meals, nothing 2–3 h before bed, raise the head of the bed) + PPI
    • CCB for Raynaud (then PDE5i, iloprost for ulcers)
    • PFT
    • pulmonary consult
    SSc-ILD
    • low-dose steroid + mycophenolate
    • if no response → tocilizumab or rituximab
    • nintedanib for progressive fibrosis
    Fever, rash and weakness
    3. Adult-onset Still disease (21F, fever and joint pain for 2 weeks)
    Clues
    • documented afternoon fever 39 not responding to antipyretics
    • sweating, fatigue
    • wrist and PIP arthritis starting with the fever
    • T 40, HR 110
    • left axillary node
    • rash (salmon-pink evanescent)
    • hepatosplenomegaly
    • dipstick negative
    DDx
    • AOSD
    • SLE
    • early RA
    • PsA
    • viral infection (hepatitis, HIV, EBV)
    • lymphoma
    • brucella
    • TB
    Workup
    • CBC (with differential)
    • renal profile
    • liver profile + albumin
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP, ferritin — glycosylated fraction, LDH)
    • immunology (ANA, RF, anti-CCP, anti-dsDNA, C3 / C4)
    • urine (urinalysis with microscopy, UPCR)
    • coagulation + aPL (MAS — fibrinogen, triglycerides)
    • infection (blood cultures, HBV, HCV, HIV, EBV, QuantiFERON, Brucella titer)
    • imaging (CXR, echo, CT chest / abdomen / pelvis)
    • node biopsy if lymphoma suspected
    Results
    • temperature 40 °C
    • WBC 13 ×10⁹/L (neutrophilia)
    • Hb 10 g/dL (100 g/L)
    • MCV 90 fL
    • platelets 400 ×10⁹/L
    • renal normal
    • AST 120, ALT 150 U/L
    • albumin and bilirubin normal
    • ESR 90 mm/h
    • CRP 50 mg/L
    • ANA, RF, anti-CCP, anti-dsDNA negative
    • C3 / C4 normal
    • ferritin 2,000 µg/L (ng/mL)
    • LDH normal
    • urine normal
    • CXR normal
    • CT hepatosplenomegaly, no lymphadenopathy
    • PT / PTT normal
    • viral serology, QuantiFERON and Brucella negative
    Diagnosis
    • adult-onset Still disease (Yamaguchi criteria — diagnosis of exclusion)
    Management
    • admit
    • rheumatology
    • prednisone 1 mg/kg/day
    • methotrexate as steroid sparing
    • IL-1 (anakinra, canakinumab) or IL-6 (tocilizumab) if refractory
    • watch for macrophage activation syndrome (falling ESR, cytopenias, ferritin rising, high TG, low fibrinogen)
    4. Dermatomyositis (42F, weakness and malaise 2 months)
    Clues
    • progressive symmetric proximal weakness (combing hair, stairs)
    • photosensitive facial rash
    • hand rash
    • mild dyspnea
    • arthralgia
    • proximal power 3/5, normal tone and reflexes, no UMN signs or fasciculation
    DDx
    • idiopathic inflammatory myopathy (dermatomyositis)
    • SLE
    • hypothyroidism
    Workup
    • muscle enzymes (CK, LDH, AST / ALT, aldolase)
    • CBC
    • electrolytes (K, Ca, Mg, PO4)
    • renal profile
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • TFT
    • imaging (CXR → HRCT and PFT for ILD; muscle MRI)
    • EMG
    • muscle biopsy
    • antibodies (myositis-specific — Jo-1, Mi-2, MDA5, TIF1-γ, NXP2, SRP, HMGCR; ANA)
    • malignancy screen (age-appropriate + CT CAP)
    • swallowing assessment
    Results
    • WBC 7.8 ×10⁹/L
    • Hb 12.5 g/dL (125 g/L)
    • platelets 310 ×10⁹/L
    • ESR 40 mm/h
    • CRP negative
    • CK 3,000 U/L (≈ 15× ULN)
    • LFT, creatinine, chemistry normal
    • TSH / T3 / T4 normal
    • CXR normal
    • proximal power 3/5 with normal reflexes
    Further
    • muscle MRI (edema)
    • EMG (membrane irritability)
    • muscle biopsy (CD4 infiltrate, capillary injury, perifascicular atrophy)
    • myositis-specific / associated antibodies (anti-Jo-1, Mi-2, MDA5, TIF1-γ, NXP2)
    Diagnosis
    • dermatomyositis
    Management
    • prednisone 1 mg/kg for 4–6 weeks then taper
    • steroid-sparing azathioprine or methotrexate (IVIG / rituximab if refractory)
    • calcium, vitamin D, PPI
    • rheumatology
    • physiotherapy
    • education
    • screen for malignancy (age-appropriate + CT CAP) and ILD (HRCT, PFT)
    5. IgA vasculitis / HSP (17M, leg rash 5 days)
    Clues
    • URTI last month
    • abdominal pain and nausea
    • knee arthralgia
    • BP 140/82
    • violaceous palpable purpura on legs and feet
    • epigastric tenderness
    • dipstick protein ++, blood +
    DDx
    • IgA vasculitis
    • SLE
    • other vasculitis
    • viral infection
    Workup
    • CBC
    • renal profile (creatinine, eGFR)
    • liver profile
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • urine (urinalysis with microscopy, 24-h protein or UPCR)
    • immunology (ANA, anti-dsDNA, ANCA, serum IgA, C3 / C4)
    • infection (HBV, HCV, HIV, ASO)
    • coagulation + smear + hemolysis panel (exclude TTP / ITP)
    • imaging (renal US with Doppler, abdominal X-ray / US for intussusception)
    • skin biopsy with immunofluorescence
    Results
    • WBC 7.1 ×10⁹/L
    • Hb 12.5 g/dL
    • platelets 210 ×10⁹/L (normal — non-thrombocytopenic purpura)
    • ESR 19 mm/h
    • CRP negative
    • ANA, anti-dsDNA, ANCA negative
    • IgA moderately raised
    • urine scanty RBC casts, 24-h protein 300 mg
    • LFT, creatinine normal
    • coagulation normal
    • abdominal and renal US normal
    • HBV, HCV, HIV negative
    • skin biopsy IgA deposits
    Diagnosis
    • IgA vasculitis (HSP) with mild IgA nephropathy
    Renal biopsy
    • not needed (normal creatinine, protein < 500 mg)
    • biopsy if rising creatinine, nephrotic range or persistent proteinuria
    Management
    • admit
    • analgesia (avoid NSAIDs with renal involvement)
    • ACEi for proteinuria
    • prednisolone 1 mg/kg for severe GI / renal disease, azathioprine as needed
    • DVT and GI prophylaxis
    • rheumatology, nephrology, dermatology
    • education
    • follow urine and BP
    6. EGPA (42F, asthma 10 years, sinus congestion, foot drop, purpura)
    Clues
    • worsening asthma and sinusitis
    • 3 months of right foot tingling and weakness
    • leg purpura
    • bilateral wheeze
    • right foot drop
    DDx
    • EGPA
    • GPA
    • MPA
    • hypereosinophilic syndrome
    • IgA vasculitis
    • PAN
    • diabetic / compressive neuropathy
    Workup
    • CBC with absolute eosinophils
    • renal profile + urinalysis with microscopy
    • liver profile
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • coagulation + smear
    • immunology (ANA, anti-dsDNA, c-ANCA / PR3, p-ANCA / MPO, IgE, IgA)
    • infection (HBV, HCV, HIV, stool for Strongyloides)
    • cardiac (troponin, ECG, echo — cardiac involvement = poor prognosis)
    • imaging (CXR, CT chest, CT sinuses)
    • PFT
    • neurophysiology (nerve conduction)
    • tissue (skin / nerve biopsy)
    Results
    • WBC 15 ×10⁹/L (15,000/µL)
    • eosinophils 4.0 ×10⁹/L (4,000/µL; > 1.5 = hypereosinophilia)
    • ESR 50 mm/h
    • CRP raised
    • MPO-ANCA (p-ANCA) positive
    • CT patchy infiltrates
    • nerve conduction axonal neuropathy (mononeuritis multiplex)
    Diagnosis
    • eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis
    Biopsy
    • when the diagnosis is uncertain, especially ANCA negative (only 30–40% are ANCA positive)
    • skin, nerve or lung
    • shows eosinophil-rich granulomatous necrotizing vasculitis
    Management
    • rheumatology, pulmonology, neurology, dermatology
    • induction high-dose steroids ± cyclophosphamide or rituximab if organ / life-threatening
    • maintenance azathioprine, methotrexate or rituximab
    • mepolizumab for relapsing / refractory non-severe disease
    • inhalers
    • PJP prophylaxis with cyclophosphamide
    Skin
    • palpable purpura (legs, commonest)
    • urticarial / erythematous rash
    • subcutaneous nodules
    • livedo
    Prognosis
    • 5-year survival > 90%
    • worse with cardiac involvement, GI vasculitis, renal impairment (Five-Factor Score)
    SLE and Sjögren
    7. Active SLE with immune thrombocytopenia (39F, fatigue and epistaxis)
    Clues
    • SLE for 2 years on HCQ only
    • fatigue 3 weeks, hair fall, arthralgia, malar rash
    • one small spontaneous nosebleed
    • malar rash, PIP arthritis
    • dipstick normal
    Workup
    • CBC + peripheral smear
    • coagulation
    • renal profile
    • liver profile + bilirubin
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • lupus activity (anti-dsDNA, C3 / C4)
    • APS screen
    • urine (urinalysis with microscopy, 24-h protein or UPCR)
    • hemolysis (LDH, retics, DAT)
    • ferritin (MAS)
    • viral screen (HIV, HCV, HBV)
    • imaging (renal Doppler US)
    Results
    • WBC 3.5 ×10⁹/L with lymphopenia
    • Hb 12.5 g/dL (125 g/L)
    • platelets 80 ×10⁹/L
    • ESR 40 mm/h
    • CRP negative
    • anti-dsDNA high
    • C3 and C4 low
    • ferritin 800 µg/L
    • urine and 24-h protein normal
    • LFT, creatinine normal
    • coagulation normal
    • smear normal (no schistocytes)
    • APS and viral serology negative
    • renal Doppler normal
    Diagnosis
    • active SLE with immune thrombocytopenia
    Activity scores
    • SLEDAI
    • BILAG
    • CLASI (skin)
    Management
    • outpatient
    • education
    • rheumatology (flare, step up immunosuppression)
    • hematology (exclude other causes)
    • steroids (prednisolone or dexamethasone)
    • IVIG if active bleeding, before surgery or steroid intolerance
    • then TPO receptor agonist, rituximab, splenectomy
    • mycophenolate
    • continue HCQ
    8. SLE with TTP and seizure (23F, class III lupus nephritis, fever and seizure)
    Clues
    • fever 39.5 for a week
    • generalized tonic-clonic seizure with tongue bite and incontinence
    • headache, confusion
    • wrist arthritis
    • dark urine
    • non-adherent
    • GCS 13, febrile, hypertensive, tachycardic
    • malar rash, pallor, mild jaundice, hepatosplenomegaly
    • dipstick protein +, blood +
    DDx
    • SLE flare with neuropsychiatric lupus
    • TTP / HUS
    • hypertensive encephalopathy / PRES
    • APS
    • CNS infection
    • metabolic
    • drug-induced seizure
    Workup
    • CBC + peripheral smear (schistocytes)
    • hemolysis panel (LDH, haptoglobin, bilirubin, retics, DAT)
    • coagulation (PT, PTT, fibrinogen, D-dimer) + ADAMTS13 activity
    • renal profile + electrolytes + glucose
    • liver profile + albumin
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP, ferritin)
    • lupus activity (anti-dsDNA, C3 / C4)
    • APS serology
    • urine (urinalysis with microscopy, UPCR, ACR)
    • infection (blood / urine cultures, CMV, EBV, HIV, HBV, HCV, TB, Brucella)
    • LP
    • imaging (MRI brain with contrast ± MRA / MRV, echo)
    • EEG
    • fundoscopy
    Diagnosis
    • thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura in a patient with SLE
    Management
    • ICU
    • neurology, rheumatology, hematology
    • ABC
    • IV benzodiazepine then levetiracetam load
    • PLASMIC score while ADAMTS13 is pending
    • urgent daily plasma exchange until platelets and LDH normalize (ADAMTS13 < 10%)
    • hold ACEi before PLEX (bradykinin → hypotension)
    • IV methylprednisolone pulses
    • rituximab / caplacizumab per hematology
    • RBC transfusion as needed, platelets only for life-threatening bleeding
    • dialysis if needed
    • control BP with CCB / beta blocker
    • continue HCQ
    • renal biopsy when safe
    9. Sjögren syndrome with distal RTA (36F, 1 day of weakness, joint pain)
    Clues
    • 9 months of small-joint hand pain and sicca
    • daughter with congenital heart block
    • HR 120, RR 25
    • bilateral non-tender parotid enlargement
    • power 2/5 proximal and distal, generalized hyporeflexia
    • no rash
    DDx
    • RA
    • autoimmune myopathy
    • Sjögren
    • neuromuscular (MG, MS)
    • hypothyroidism
    • hypokalemic paralysis
    Exam add-ons
    • ECG (U waves, ST depression, long QT)
    • mouth dryness and caries
    • Schirmer test
    • parotids
    • nodes
    Workup
    • electrolytes (Na, K, Ca, Mg, Cl → anion gap)
    • blood gas (VBG — pH, HCO3)
    • urine (pH, urine anion gap, urinalysis)
    • renal profile
    • CK
    • CBC
    • liver profile
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • TSH
    • immunology (ANA, anti-SSA / Ro, anti-SSB / La, RF, anti-CCP, anti-Jo-1, C3 / C4, SPEP, cryoglobulins)
    • ECG
    • neurophysiology (EMG / NCS)
    • minor salivary gland biopsy
    • renal US (nephrocalcinosis)
    Results
    • HR 120, RR 25
    • CBC normal
    • ESR 40 mm/h
    • CRP positive
    • LFT and renal normal
    • CK and TSH normal
    • K 1.9 mmol/L (1.9 mEq/L)
    • VBG normal anion gap (hyperchloremic) metabolic acidosis
    • SSA and SSB positive
    • ANA and RF negative
    • ECG ST depression, U waves, long QT
    • lip biopsy lymphocytic infiltration
    Diagnosis
    • primary Sjögren syndrome with distal (type 1) RTA and hypokalemic paralysis
    Management
    • admit (cardiac monitor)
    • rheumatology and nephrology
    • IV potassium (correct K before bicarbonate)
    • oral alkali (potassium citrate)
    • corticosteroids
    • methotrexate or rituximab if indicated
    • artificial tears and saliva, pilocarpine
    Lymphoma risk factors
    • persistent parotid enlargement
    • lymphadenopathy
    • splenomegaly
    • cryoglobulins
    • positive RF
    • low C4
    • purpura / vasculitis
    Renal complications
    • tubulointerstitial nephritis, distal RTA, glomerulonephritis (MPGN with cryoglobulins), CKD
    • also nephrogenic DI
    Pregnancy with anti-Ro / La
    • congenital heart block (1–5%) and neonatal lupus
    • serial fetal echo / PR interval weeks 16–26
    • HCQ reduces CHB risk
    • continue safe drugs
    • preconception planning
    • neonatal check after delivery
    Inflammatory arthritis
    10. Seropositive rheumatoid arthritis (40F, 2 months of small-joint pain)
    History asks
    • additive vs migratory pattern
    • number, symmetry, distribution
    • morning stiffness
    • NSAID response
    • inflammatory back pain, dactylitis, enthesitis
    • deformity
    • constitutional
    • psoriasis, malar rash, Raynaud, ulcers, sicca, red eyes
    • cough / dyspnea
    • IBD symptoms
    • hypothyroidism
    • drugs
    • family history
    • miscarriages
    • smoking, occupation
    Clues
    • symmetric additive MCP / PIP / wrist arthritis
    • 2-hour morning stiffness
    • partial response to diclofenac
    • smoker
    • hypothyroid
    • no deformity, rash or nodules
    DDx
    • RA
    • PsA
    • SLE arthritis
    • viral (parvovirus)
    • hypothyroid arthropathy
    Workup
    • CBC
    • renal profile
    • liver profile (before methotrexate)
    • electrolytes
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • immunology (RF, anti-CCP, ANA, anti-dsDNA, C3 / C4)
    • TFT
    • vitamin D, uric acid
    • imaging (X-ray hands / wrists / feet; US / MRI for synovitis)
    • pre-DMARD screen (HBV, HCV, HIV, TB — IGRA / PPD, CXR)
    Results
    • ESR 65 mm/h
    • CRP high
    • RF positive
    • anti-CCP strongly positive
    • ANA negative
    • CBC, renal, LFT normal
    • X-ray early periarticular osteopenia, no erosions
    Diagnosis
    • seropositive rheumatoid arthritis
    Management
    • education, stop smoking, rheumatology
    • physio / OT
    • vaccines (influenza, pneumococcal, HBV)
    • NSAIDs and short low-dose steroid bridge
    • methotrexate 10–15 mg weekly escalating with folic acid (alternatives leflunomide, sulfasalazine, HCQ)
    • biologic (anti-TNF) or JAK inhibitor if inadequate response
    • treat to target (remission / low activity, DAS28 every 1–3 months)
    • pre-treatment screening and baseline CBC, LFT, renal
    • manage CV risk
    Most specific antibody
    • anti-CCP
    Poor prognosis
    • many joints (> 6)
    • high RF / anti-CCP titers
    • early erosions
    • extra-articular disease
    • high ESR / CRP
    • poor function
    Extra-articular
    • nodules
    • episcleritis / scleritis
    • sicca
    • ILD
    • pleural effusion
    • pericarditis
    • vasculitis
    • Felty syndrome (RA + splenomegaly + neutropenia)
    • anemia
    • amyloidosis
    Methotrexate toxicity
    • hepatotoxicity
    • marrow suppression
    • pneumonitis
    • mucositis
    • teratogenic
    Start biologics
    • no improvement by 3 months or target not reached by 6 months on csDMARDs (especially with poor prognostic factors)
    11. Psoriatic arthritis (21M, psoriasis 3 years, low back pain 6 months)
    Clues
    • morning back pain with 1-hour stiffness, better with activity, radiating to the right buttock
    • right 3rd / 4th DIP arthritis
    • active psoriatic plaques on elbows
    • nail pitting
    • positive FABER and Gaenslen
    • BMI 30
    DDx
    • PsA
    • AS
    • reactive arthritis
    • brucellosis
    • TB
    • HIV-associated
    Workup
    • CBC
    • renal profile
    • liver profile + albumin
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • immunology (ANA, RF, anti-CCP, anti-dsDNA, HLA-B27)
    • urinalysis
    • imaging (X-ray hands; X-ray SI joints — Ferguson view; MRI SI joints; CXR; echo)
    • comorbidity (lipid profile, glucose, uric acid)
    • infection (HBV, HCV, HIV, EBV, QuantiFERON, Brucella titer)
    Results
    • WBC 8 ×10⁹/L
    • Hb 14 g/dL
    • platelets 400 ×10⁹/L
    • renal and LFT normal
    • ESR 90 mm/h
    • CRP 50 mg/L
    • ANA, RF, anti-CCP, anti-dsDNA negative
    • urine normal
    • CXR normal
    • hand X-ray pencil-in-cup DIP erosions
    • MRI active sacroiliitis
    • viral serology, QuantiFERON and Brucella negative
    • BMI 30
    Diagnosis
    • psoriatic arthritis (axial + DIP)
    Management
    • outpatient
    • rheumatology and dermatology
    • physio and weight loss
    • NSAIDs
    • methotrexate with folic acid (peripheral disease), leflunomide or apremilast
    • anti-TNF (etanercept, adalimumab, infliximab)
    • IL-17 (secukinumab, ixekizumab)
    • IL-12/23 (ustekinumab)
    • for axial disease csDMARDs don't work — go to anti-TNF / IL-17
    12. Ankylosing spondylitis (27M resident, back pain > 3 months) — with biologic counseling
    History asks
    • back pain analysis
    • weakness, numbness, sphincters
    • other joints, dactylitis, enthesitis
    • fever, weight loss
    • rash, oral / genital ulcers, eye symptoms
    • recent infection
    • IBD
    • TB contact
    • raw milk
    • family psoriasis
    • NSAIDs
    • trauma
    • sexual history, smoking
    • work / sport
    Clues
    • inflammatory back pain with > 30 min stiffness improving with activity
    • no extra-articular features
    Exam
    • vitals, BMI, eyes, skin / mucosa (psoriasis, keratoderma, balanitis, nails), joints, enthesitis, dactylitis
    • full back exam (section 4)
    • neuro exam
    DDx
    • AS
    • reactive arthritis
    • PsA
    • IBD arthropathy
    • brucellosis
    Workup
    • CBC
    • liver profile
    • renal profile + urinalysis
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • HLA-B27
    • infection (Brucella titer, HBV, HCV, HIV, QuantiFERON — before anti-TNF)
    • imaging (X-ray SI joints → MRI SI joints)
    Results
    • SI X-ray shows ankylosis of the SI joints
    Diagnosis
    • ankylosing spondylitis (radiographic axial SpA)
    Management
    • outpatient
    • rheumatology
    • NSAIDs (2 for 4 weeks each)
    • anti-TNF if NSAIDs fail
    • IL-17 or JAK inhibitors
    • physiotherapy and exercise
    Biologic counseling
    • infection and TB reactivation risk
    • SC every 2 weeks (adalimumab)
    • dose, duration and injection technique
    • allergy and side effects with leaflet
    • ask about HF, lung disease, demyelination, thrombosis (JAKi), malignancy
    • vaccines before starting (influenza, pneumococcal, recombinant zoster)
    • pregnancy / breastfeeding
    13. Axial SpA with back exam (26M, morning stiffness > 1 hour for 3 years)
    Task
    • back exam (section 4)
    • then DDx AS
    • PsA
    • reactive arthritis
    • IBD arthropathy
    Workup
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • immunology (RF, ACPA, HLA-B27)
    • imaging (X-ray SI joints, lumbar and thoracic spine → MRI SI joints and thoracolumbar spine)
    Modified New York criteria (asked as "ASAS")
    • clinical — low back pain ≥ 3 months improving with exercise not rest
    • limited lumbar movement in sagittal and frontal planes
    • reduced chest expansion for age / sex
    • radiologic — bilateral grade 2–4 or unilateral grade 3–4 sacroiliitis
    • AS = radiologic + ≥ 1 clinical
    ASAS axial SpA (for completeness)
    • back pain ≥ 3 months, onset < 45
    • sacroiliitis on imaging + ≥ 1 SpA feature, or HLA-B27 + ≥ 2 SpA features
    Management
    • education
    • NSAIDs
    • rheumatology
    • TNF inhibitor, IL-17 inhibitor, JAK inhibitor
    14. Ankylosing spondylitis — non-radiographic start (21M, chronic back pain)
    Clues
    • 3 months of morning pain with 1-hour stiffness improving with activity
    • no other joints, psoriasis, infection, TB contact or raw milk
    Workup
    • CBC
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • renal profile + urinalysis
    • liver profile
    • immunology (HLA-B27, ANA, RF, anti-CCP)
    • infection (TB, Brucella, HBV, HCV)
    • imaging (X-ray SI joints, MRI SI joints, CXR, echo for AR)
    Results
    • high inflammatory markers
    • seronegative
    • infections negative
    • MRI SI bone marrow edema, more on the right = active sacroiliitis
    Diagnosis
    • ankylosing spondylitis (axial SpA)
    Management
    • education and exercise / physiotherapy
    • NSAIDs
    • anti-TNF or IL-17 if NSAIDs fail
    • screen before biologics
    Questions asked across several stations
    Pre-biologic checklist
    • TB (IGRA / TST + CXR), HBV / HCV / HIV, CBC, LFT, renal
    • vaccines (influenza, pneumococcal, recombinant zoster, no live vaccines during therapy)
    • contraindications (active infection, HF for anti-TNF, demyelination, malignancy, thrombosis for JAKi, pregnancy)
    MAHA in a CTD patient
    • schistocytes + low platelets → think TTP (ADAMTS13)
    • HUS
    • scleroderma renal crisis (very high BP)
    • catastrophic APS
    • malignant HTN
    Antibody associations
    • Scl-70 = diffuse SSc + ILD
    • anticentromere = limited SSc + PAH
    • RNA polymerase III = renal crisis
    • anti-Jo-1 = antisynthetase (ILD, mechanic's hands)
    • anti-CCP = RA
    • SSA / SSB = Sjögren, neonatal lupus / CHB
    • anti-dsDNA and low complement = active SLE / nephritis
    Seronegative SpA features
    • inflammatory back pain
    • enthesitis
    • dactylitis
    • uveitis
    • psoriasis
    • IBD
    • reactive (post GI / GU infection)
    • HLA-B27
    Always exclude in Saudi stations
    • brucellosis (raw milk) and TB before calling it autoimmune
    6. Data interpretation

    Images from the checklists, grouped by station, with the finding and the diagnosis.

    Hands — 35F with SSc, bluish fingers and headache (case 1)
    Findings
    • dusky blue-purple discoloration of the fingers (cyanotic phase of Raynaud)
    • tight shiny skin with loss of skin creases (sclerodactyly)
    Diagnosis
    • Raynaud phenomenon secondary to systemic sclerosis
    Fundoscopy — same patient, BP 210/130 (case 1)
    Findings
    • widespread flame-shaped hemorrhages
    • cotton-wool spots
    • hard exudates and optic disc swelling
    Diagnosis
    • grade IV (Keith-Wagener) hypertensive retinopathy = malignant hypertension → scleroderma renal crisis
    Peripheral blood film — same patient (case 1)
    Findings
    • fragmented red cells (schistocytes / helmet cells, arrows)
    • platelets 90 ×10⁹/L
    Diagnosis
    • microangiopathic hemolytic anemia of scleroderma renal crisis → captopril
    Skin — 21F, fever 39–40 °C and arthritis (case 3)
    Findings
    • evanescent salmon-pink macular / maculopapular rash on the trunk
    • appearing with fever spikes
    Diagnosis
    • Still rash → adult-onset Still disease (ferritin 2000 µg/L)
    Eyelids — 42F, proximal weakness (case 4)
    Findings
    • violaceous / purplish erythema and edema of the upper eyelid
    Diagnosis
    • heliotrope rash → dermatomyositis
    Hands — same patient (case 4)
    Findings
    • violaceous scaly papules over the MCP and interphalangeal joints
    Diagnosis
    • Gottron papules → dermatomyositis (CK 3000 U/L)
    Legs — 17M, rash, abdominal pain, arthralgia after URTI (case 5)
    Findings
    • palpable purpura (raised violaceous papules and plaques) on the legs and ankles
    • gravity-dependent
    Diagnosis
    • small-vessel leukocytoclastic vasculitis → IgA vasculitis (HSP)
    Skin biopsy, direct immunofluorescence — same patient (case 5)
    Findings
    • granular IgA deposits in the walls of dermal small vessels
    Diagnosis
    • IgA vasculitis (Henoch-Schönlein purpura)
    Legs — 42F, asthma, sinusitis, foot drop (case 6)
    Findings
    • palpable purpura and petechiae on the lower legs and feet
    Diagnosis
    • cutaneous small-vessel vasculitis → EGPA (eosinophils 4,000/µL, MPO-ANCA positive)
    Skin — 39F with SLE, fatigue and epistaxis (case 7)
    Findings
    • scattered non-blanching red petechiae and small purpuric macules on the limbs
    Diagnosis
    • thrombocytopenic purpura (platelets 80 ×10⁹/L) → immune thrombocytopenia in active SLE
    Peripheral blood film — 23F with lupus nephritis, fever and seizure (case 8)
    Findings
    • helmet cells and fragmented red cells (arrows)
    • microspherocytes (large arrowheads)
    • reduced platelets with a large platelet (dashed arrow)
    Diagnosis
    • microangiopathic hemolytic anemia → TTP in SLE
    Normal blood film for comparison (case 8)
    Findings
    • uniform round red cells with central pallor one-third of the diameter
    • normal platelets (arrowheads)
    • small lymphocyte (arrow)
    Diagnosis
    • normal peripheral smear
    ECG — 36F with sicca symptoms, generalized weakness (case 9)
    Findings
    • sinus tachycardia
    • diffuse ST-segment depression
    • flattened T waves with prominent U waves
    • prolonged QT (QU) interval
    Diagnosis
    • severe hypokalemia (K 1.9 mmol/L = 1.9 mEq/L) from distal RTA in Sjögren syndrome
    Elbows — 21M with psoriasis, inflammatory back pain (case 11)
    Findings
    • well-demarcated erythematous plaques with thick silvery scale on the extensor elbows
    Diagnosis
    • chronic plaque psoriasis
    Nails — same patient (case 11)
    Findings
    • multiple nail pits
    • ridging and onycholysis
    Diagnosis
    • psoriatic nail disease — strongly linked to DIP arthritis in PsA
    Hand X-ray — same patient (case 11)
    Findings
    • DIP erosions with "pencil-in-cup" deformity (arrows), joint space loss, periostitis
    • no periarticular osteopenia
    Diagnosis
    • psoriatic arthritis
    Pelvis X-ray — 27M, inflammatory low back pain > 3 months (case 12)
    Findings
    • bilateral sacroiliac joint sclerosis
    • erosions and joint-space narrowing / partial ankylosis (grade 3 — "unequivocal abnormality")
    Diagnosis
    • bilateral sacroiliitis → ankylosing spondylitis (radiographic axial SpA; modified New York criteria met)
    MRI sacroiliac joints (STIR) — 21M, chronic inflammatory back pain (case 14)
    Findings
    • periarticular bone marrow edema (high STIR signal, arrows) in the sacrum and ilium
    • more on the right
    Diagnosis
    • active sacroiliitis → axial spondyloarthritis / ankylosing spondylitis

    General & Communication

    How to use this

    The 6 general stations are communication stations with a medical tail: half the marks (≈ 50) are for the communication framework, the rest for examiner questions. Learn the three frameworks below once; they also reappear in other specialties (gentamicin ATN, warfarin, MTC, esophageal cancer).

    Stations covered
    • breaking bad news ×2 (lung cancer, colon cancer)
    • angry patient with drug reaction ×2 (amoxicillin, ibuprofen → anaphylaxis)
    • medication counseling ×2 (biologic for RA, cyclophosphamide for lupus nephritis)
    1. Framework: breaking bad news (SPIKES)
    Setting
    • introduce yourself and role
    • confirm name and age
    • explain the purpose
    • consent
    • private room, phone off, door closed
    • sit down
    • ask if they want a relative present
    Perception
    • ask what they already know or suspect
    • ideas, concerns, expectations, fears
    Invitation
    • ask how much they want to know and whether they want the result today
    Knowledge
    • warning shot ("I'm afraid the results are not what we hoped")
    • explain why the test was done
    • give the result clearly in plain words
    • avoid jargon
    • small chunks
    Emotions
    • allow silence
    • acknowledge and name the emotion
    • listen without interrupting
    • eye contact
    • tissue / water
    Strategy
    • outline the plan and MDT (oncology, surgery, radiotherapy, palliative care, psychology, social work)
    • explore physical and psychosocial support
    • arrange follow-up
    Safety and close
    • offer help getting home or a family meeting
    • contact numbers
    • leaflets, websites, support groups
    • check understanding
    • invite questions
    • summarize
    • thank
    2. Framework: angry patient after an adverse event
    First
    • say you will make sure the patient is stable (ABC) before talking
    Introduction
    • introduce
    • confirm identity
    • explain why you are there
    • consent
    Safety
    • door open
    • sit nearer the door
    • not too close
    • invite the patient to sit
    Emotions
    • find the reason for anger
    • let him vent without interrupting
    • acknowledge empathically and non-judgmentally
    • calm tone, simple language
    • eye contact
    Apology
    • apologize and accept the grievance
    • explain the aim is to treat him properly
    • never blame a colleague, department or service
    • not defensive
    Actions
    • share the plan for the current problem
    • promise a transparent investigation to prevent recurrence
    • explain how to make a formal complaint
    • reassure that complaining will not affect his care
    Close
    • follow-up plan
    • how to reach help
    • resources
    • check understanding
    • questions
    • thank
    3. Framework: medication counseling
    Introduction
    • introduce
    • confirm identity
    • purpose
    • consent
    • private setting
    • relative present?
    Explore
    • what she knows
    • ideas, concerns, expectations
    • current disease activity and treatment
    • adherence and reasons for failure
    • comorbidities (liver, kidney, heart, lung)
    Explain
    • the diagnosis in plain words
    • why this drug
    • name, route, dose, schedule
    • alternatives with their pros and cons
    Risks
    • common and serious side effects
    • how they will be reduced (monitoring, prophylaxis)
    Safety net
    • when to stop and call (fever, rash, jaundice, bleeding, pregnancy)
    • contact numbers
    • leaflet
    Close
    • check understanding
    • questions
    • consent
    • thank
    4. Case scenarios

    Each case: stem → communication task → examiner's questions.

    Breaking bad news
    1. Lung cancer (73M, heavy smoker, nodule on CXR, biopsy positive)
    Task
    • SPIKES (section 1)
    Lung nodule DDx
    • malignant (primary lung cancer, metastasis, lymphoma, carcinoid, sarcoma)
    • benign tumor (hamartoma, fibroma)
    • infection (TB, fungal — histoplasma, cocci, crypto, aspergilloma; abscess; hydatid)
    • inflammatory (sarcoid, RA nodule, GPA)
    • vascular (AVM, infarct)
    • congenital (bronchogenic cyst, sequestration)
    Complications of lung cancer
    • SVC syndrome
    • Pancoast tumor with Horner syndrome / brachial plexus
    • hoarseness (recurrent laryngeal nerve)
    • pleural effusion
    • post-obstructive pneumonia
    • metastases
    • thrombosis
    • paraneoplastic (SIADH, hypercalcemia, Lambert-Eaton, Cushing)
    • death
    Solitary pulmonary nodule
    • single
    • < 3 cm
    • surrounded by normal lung
    • no lymphadenopathy
    • no effusion
    2. Colon cancer (50M, rectal bleeding and weight loss)
    Task
    • SPIKES (section 1)
    Risk factors
    • age > 45
    • IBD
    • personal / family history of CRC or polyps
    • low-fiber high-fat diet with red / processed meat
    • inactivity
    • DM
    • obesity
    • male sex
    • alcohol
    • smoking
    Saudi high-risk group
    • prior polyps
    • ≥ 1 first-degree or ≥ 2 second-degree relatives with CRC / polyps
    • UC or Crohn's
    • prior abdominal / pelvic radiation
    Prognostic factors
    • stage (I–II better)
    • node involvement
    • grade and histology
    • metastases
    • molecular markers (KRAS / BRAF worse; MSI-high / dMMR responds to immunotherapy)
    • performance status (ECOG, Karnofsky)
    • response to treatment
    Angry patient with drug reaction → anaphylaxis
    3. Amoxicillin given despite penicillin allergy (62M, leg cellulitis)
    Task
    • angry patient framework (section 2)
    Leg swelling DDx
    • infection (cellulitis, abscess, necrotizing fasciitis)
    • DVT / thrombophlebitis / post-thrombotic
    • venous or arterial insufficiency
    • lymphedema
    • ruptured Baker cyst
    • hematoma, compartment syndrome
    • myositis / rhabdomyolysis
    • tumor
    Turns into
    • severe skin reaction
    • SOB
    • dizziness = anaphylaxis
    4. Ibuprofen given despite aspirin allergy (60M, knee OA)
    Task
    • angry patient framework (section 2)
    Flushing DDx
    • anaphylaxis
    • carcinoid
    • pheochromocytoma
    • mastocytosis
    • medullary thyroid cancer
    • VIPoma
    • serotonin syndrome
    • menopause / emotional / autonomic
    • rosacea
    • drugs (niacin, CCB, vancomycin, steroids)
    • alcohol
    • spicy food
    Turns into
    • anaphylactic (distributive) shock
    Anaphylaxis management (both stations)
    Immediate
    • ABC
    • stop the trigger
    • IM adrenaline 0.5 mg (1:1000) in the thigh, repeat every 5 min
    Airway and breathing
    • high-flow O2
    • salbutamol for wheeze
    • intubation or cricothyrotomy for laryngeal edema
    Circulation
    • lie flat, legs up
    • large-volume IV fluids
    • adrenaline infusion / vasopressors in ICU if refractory
    • glucagon if on beta blockers
    Adjuncts
    • IV antihistamine (diphenhydramine / chlorphenamine)
    • IV steroid (hydrocortisone) to reduce biphasic reaction
    After
    • observe / admit
    • allergy referral
    • document allergy
    • education and adrenaline auto-injector
    Medication counseling
    5. Biologic therapy for RA not controlled on methotrexate (26F)
    Explore
    • disease duration and activity (joint count, morning stiffness, extra-articular features)
    • doses and adherence
    • reasons MTX failed (smoking, interactions, fibromyalgia, OSA)
    Infection screen
    • recent infection
    • TB history or symptoms (cough, hemoptysis, night sweats, weight loss)
    • shingles
    • HIV, hepatitis B / C and their risk factors
    Contraindications to ask
    • heart failure
    • demyelinating disease
    • malignancy
    • lung disease
    • thrombosis (JAKi)
    • pregnancy / breastfeeding
    Vaccines before starting
    • influenza
    • pneumococcal
    • recombinant zoster (live vaccines before, not during)
    Educate
    • dose, schedule, injection technique
    • infection and TB reactivation risk
    • allergy
    • when to report
    • leaflet
    • consent
    Pre-biologic tests
    • CBC (with differential)
    • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
    • renal profile (creatinine, eGFR)
    • liver profile
    • lipid profile (tocilizumab, JAKi)
    • urinalysis
    • infection screen (HBV — HBsAg, anti-HBc; HCV; HIV; latent TB — TST or QuantiFERON)
    • imaging (CXR)
    Approved biologics / targeted drugs
    • TNF inhibitors (infliximab, etanercept, adalimumab, certolizumab, golimumab)
    • IL-6 (tocilizumab, sarilumab)
    • rituximab
    • abatacept
    • JAK inhibitors (tofacitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib)
    6. Cyclophosphamide for lupus nephritis class III with crescents (30F)
    Explore
    • SLE course and activity, renal and volume symptoms, obstetric history, thrombosis
    • current drugs and adherence
    • NSAIDs
    • comorbidities
    Regimens
    • Euro-Lupus low dose = 500 mg IV every 2 weeks × 6
    • NIH high dose = 0.5–1 g/m² IV monthly × 6
    Side effects
    • infertility / premature ovarian failure (higher if > 30 years)
    • marrow suppression and infection
    • hemorrhagic cystitis and bladder cancer
    • teratogenic
    • SIADH
    • nausea, alopecia
    Risk reduction
    • GnRH agonist (leuprolide 3.75 mg IM 10–14 days before each dose)
    • IV hydration
    • MESNA
    • antiemetics (ondansetron, dexamethasone)
    • PCP prophylaxis with TMP-SMX
    • contraception
    Stop and call if
    • rash / allergy
    • fever or infection
    • severe vomiting
    • jaundice
    • blood in urine
    • planning pregnancy
    High dose preferred when
    • adverse clinical features (nephritic sediment with GFR 25–80) or histology (crescents / necrosis in > 25% of glomeruli)
    Induction
    • cyclophosphamide or MMF
    • maintenance: MMF or azathioprine
    Antibody tracking activity / relapse
    • anti-C1q (also anti-dsDNA with low complement)
    5. Physical examination stations

    The "Examination – General" file is a collection of system-exam stations. Most already sit in their specialty tab; the lymphoreticular exam is new and kept here.

    Where each exam lives
    • cardiac exam → Cardiology
    • respiratory exam (front / back) → Pulmonology
    • abdominal exam with chronic-diarrhea questions → Gastroenterology
    • thyroid exam → Endocrinology
    • cranial nerves and extrapyramidal exam → Neurology
    • hand, back, knee, shoulder and hip exams → Rheumatology
    Lymphoreticular examination
    Start
    • wash hands
    • introduce
    • confirm name and DOB
    • explain
    • chaperone
    • consent
    • bed at 45°
    • expose
    • ask about pain
    General inspection
    • pallor, bruising / bleeding, cachexia, jaundice, scratch marks
    • surroundings (medications, oxygen, sputum pot)
    Cervical nodes (from behind, patient sitting, chin slightly down)
    • submental → submandibular → tonsillar / angle of jaw → preauricular → posterior auricular → anterior cervical chain down to the clavicle → posterior cervical chain up to the mastoid → occipital → supraclavicular and infraclavicular (tilt head to each side; left supraclavicular = Virchow node)
    Axillary nodes (lying at 45°)
    • inspect for scars or masses
    • support the patient's forearm with your same-side hand so the muscles relax
    • palpate anterior (pectoral), central (medial), posterior (subscapular), lateral (humeral) and apical groups
    • repeat on the other side
    Epitrochlear nodes
    • hold the wrist
    • fingers behind the olecranon, thumb just above the medial epicondyle (generalized lymphadenopathy, melanoma of the arm, secondary syphilis)
    Inguinal nodes (lying flat)
    • horizontal group just below the inguinal ligament (ASIS → pubic tubercle)
    • vertical group along the proximal great saphenous vein, about 3 cm lateral to the pubic tubercle
    Abdomen
    • inspect
    • light and deep palpation
    • liver
    • spleen (start in the right iliac fossa, roll onto the right side)
    Describe each node
    • site
    • size (cm)
    • number
    • consistency (hard = cancer, rubbery = lymphoma, tender soft = infection)
    • tenderness
    • mobility / fixed
    • matted
    • overlying skin
    Close
    • thank
    • summarize
    • suggest CBC with differential, blood film, LDH, HIV / EBV / CMV / TB tests, imaging (US / CT), excisional node biopsy
    Lymphadenopathy DDx
    • infection (viral — EBV, CMV, HIV; TB; brucella; toxoplasma; bacterial)
    • malignancy (lymphoma, leukemia, metastases)
    • autoimmune (SLE, RA, Still disease, sarcoidosis)
    • drugs (phenytoin, allopurinol, DRESS)
    • others (Kikuchi, Castleman)

    Miscellaneous

    How to use this

    The miscellaneous set is mostly communication stations (≈ 50 marks for the framework + 50 for examiner questions). Six of its stations are the same as the General & Communication file, so only the new ones are written out in full here; the frameworks are repeated in short form so this tab stands alone.

    Stations covered
    • angry patient — missed DVT prophylaxis ×2
    • angry patient — warfarin given by mistake
    • pre-op cardiac assessment for cholecystectomy after DES
    • plus the shared stations (breaking bad news for lung and colon cancer, anaphylaxis after amoxicillin and ibuprofen, biologic counseling in RA, cyclophosphamide counseling in lupus nephritis)
    1. Communication frameworks (short form)
    Angry patient
    • ABC first if unwell → introduce
    • confirm identity
    • purpose
    • consent → safety (door open, sit near the door, not too close) → find the reason
    • let him vent
    • acknowledge empathically
    • listen without interrupting → apologize
    • accept the grievance
    • never blame a colleague or department
    • not defensive → plan for the current problem
    • transparent investigation to prevent recurrence
    • explain the formal complaint route
    • reassure care won't be affected → follow-up
    • contact route
    • resources → check understanding
    • questions
    • thank
    Breaking bad news (SPIKES)
    • private setting
    • phone off
    • relative? → perception (ideas, concerns, expectations) → invitation (how much to know) → knowledge (warning shot, why the test, result in plain words) → emotions (silence, acknowledge) → strategy (MDT plan, psychosocial support, follow-up) → safety net (help getting home, contact number, leaflets) → check understanding
    • summarize
    • thank
    Medication counseling
    • introduce
    • consent
    • private
    • relative? → explore knowledge
    • ICE
    • disease course
    • adherence
    • comorbidities → explain diagnosis
    • drug name
    • route
    • regimen
    • alternatives → side effects and how they are reduced → when to stop and call → contacts
    • leaflets → check understanding
    • consent
    • thank
    2. Case scenarios
    Angry patient after a medical error
    1. Missed DVT prophylaxis (70M bedbound with pneumonia, new right leg swelling day 3)
    Task
    • angry patient framework
    Leg swelling DDx (8)
    • infection (cellulitis, abscess)
    • lymphedema
    • vascular (DVT, superficial thrombophlebitis, hematoma, venous or arterial insufficiency, post-thrombotic syndrome)
    • inflammatory (myositis, myonecrosis, rhabdomyolysis)
    • trauma (compartment syndrome, sport injury)
    • joint (arthritis, ruptured Baker cyst)
    • neurogenic (neuropathy)
    • tumor (lipoma, hemangioma, nerve sheath tumor)
    Result
    • Doppler shows right leg DVT
    Thrombophilia workup indications
    • recurrent VTE
    • VTE at a young age (< 45–50)
    • strong family history of VTE
    • thrombosis at unusual sites (cerebral, splanchnic, hepatic veins)
    • also recurrent pregnancy loss, warfarin skin necrosis
    Anticoagulate superficial vein thrombosis when
    • ≥ 5 cm long (extensive)
    • within 3–5 cm of the deep system (saphenofemoral / saphenopopliteal junction)
    • plus positive D-dimer, active cancer, prior VTE, above the knee, symptoms progressing (fondaparinux 2.5 mg for 45 days)
    DVT + brain metastasis
    • anticoagulation is generally safe (except hemorrhagic metastases — melanoma, RCC, choriocarcinoma, thyroid)
    • choice LMWH (enoxaparin) — DOACs acceptable in many cancers
    • duration indefinite while the cancer is active / until cured (at least 3–6 months)
    2. Missed DVT prophylaxis — repeat station
    Same stem and questions as case 1.
      Extra question
      • is it safe to anticoagulate with brain metastasis? → yes
      • enoxaparin
      • indefinite
      3. Warfarin given by mistake for 2 days (55M admitted with pneumonia, gum bleeding)
      Task
      • angry patient framework
      Bleeding gum DDx (8)
      • gingivitis / periodontitis
      • hard brushing / trauma
      • dental infection
      • leukemia
      • coagulation defects (hemophilia, liver disease, DIC)
      • platelet disorders (ITP, TTP, HUS)
      • vitamin C (scurvy) or vitamin K deficiency
      • drugs (anticoagulants, antiplatelets)
      • pregnancy
      Vitamin K or 4-factor PCC?
      • No — no significant bleeding (minor gum bleeding)
      • stop warfarin and monitor. Vitamin K (oral) if INR > 10 without bleeding
      • IV vitamin K + 4F-PCC for major bleeding
      Recheck INR
      • in 24–48 hours
      PCC complications
      • thrombosis (≈ 1.4%)
      • heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (contains heparin)
      • allergic reactions
      • infection risk (theoretical)
      PCC over FFP
      • faster INR reversal
      • small volume (less overload)
      • no need for ABO / thawing
      • lower TRALI and transfusion-reaction risk
      Drugs increasing warfarin effect
      • amiodarone
      • macrolides
      • fluoroquinolones
      • cotrimoxazole (also metronidazole, fluconazole)
      Drugs decreasing warfarin effect
      • rifampin
      • carbamazepine
      • phenytoin
      • OCPs (also vitamin K-rich food)
      Pre-operative assessment
      4. Cholecystectomy after recent STEMI with DES (64M, DM, HTN)
      History
      • timing of MI
      • PCI with stent number, site, type (BMS vs DES)
      • current cardiac symptoms (chest pain, dyspnea, orthopnea, PND, palpitations, syncope)
      • comorbidity (DM, HTN, smoking, lipids, PVD, stroke, CKD)
      • drugs (ASA, clopidogrel, beta blocker, CCB, insulin)
      • functional capacity
      Exam
      • vitals
      • JVP and leg edema
      • precordium for gallop and murmurs
      • chest for crackles
      Findings
      • STEMI 5 months ago with one DES in the LAD
      • asymptomatic
      • DM on insulin
      • ex-smoker
      • on ASA, clopidogrel, bisoprolol, lisinopril, atorvastatin, insulin
      • climbs 2 flights and jogs 30 min (≥ 4 METs)
      • exam normal
      Risk tool
      • Revised Cardiac Risk Index (Lee)
      RCRI items
      • high-risk surgery (intraperitoneal, intrathoracic, suprainguinal vascular)
      • IHD
      • HF
      • cerebrovascular disease
      • DM on insulin
      • creatinine > 2 mg/dL (177 µmol/L)
      His risk
      • high (IHD + insulin-treated DM + intraperitoneal surgery)
      Pre-op tests
      • cardiac (ECG; echo if none within a year)
      • imaging (CXR)
      • CBC
      • coagulation (PT / INR, aPTT)
      • renal profile (creatinine — RCRI cut-off > 2 mg/dL = 177 µmol/L)
      • glucose / HbA1c
      Results
      • ECG Q waves V1–V2
      • echo anteroseptal hypokinesia, EF 55%, normal PA pressure
      Anticipated complications
      • stent thrombosis
      • ACS
      • death
      • stroke
      • pulmonary edema
      • arrhythmia
      Recommendation
      • elective surgery → postpone until 12 months after DES (minimum 6 months per newer guidance) on DAPT
      When surgery goes ahead
      • monitored post-op bed
      • cardiology consult
      • stop clopidogrel 5 days before
      • continue aspirin
      • continue beta blocker and statin (hold ACEi the morning of surgery per most guidance)
      • avoid volume overload
      • restart P2Y12 as soon as bleeding risk allows
      Post-op monitoring
      • daily ECG
      • troponin (± NT-proBNP / BNP) on days 1–3
      Shared with General & Communication (see that tab for detail)
      5. Breaking bad news — lung cancer (73M heavy smoker)
      Task
      • SPIKES
      Examiner Qs
      • lung nodule DDx (malignant, benign tumor, infection, inflammatory, congenital, vascular)
      • 8 complications of lung cancer (SVC syndrome, Pancoast / Horner, hoarseness, effusion, post-obstructive pneumonia, metastases, thrombosis, paraneoplastic, death)
      • solitary nodule features (single, < 3 cm, surrounded by lung, no nodes, no effusion)
      6. Breaking bad news — colon cancer (50M, rectal bleeding, weight loss)
      Task
      • SPIKES
      Examiner Qs
      • risk factors (age > 45, IBD, family / personal CRC or polyps, low fiber / red meat, inactivity, DM, obesity, male, alcohol, smoking)
      • Saudi high-risk group (polyps, ≥ 1 first-degree or ≥ 2 second-degree relatives, IBD, prior abdominal / pelvic radiation)
      • prognostic factors (stage, nodes, grade, metastases, KRAS / BRAF vs MSI-high, performance status, treatment response)
      7. Anaphylaxis — amoxicillin despite penicillin allergy (62M, leg cellulitis)
      Task
      • ABC first
      • then angry patient framework
      Examiner Qs
      • 5 leg-swelling DDx
      • diagnosis = anaphylaxis
      Management
      • ABC
      • IM adrenaline 0.5 mg (1:1000), repeat every 5 min
      • O2, salbutamol, intubation / cricothyrotomy for laryngeal edema
      • large-volume IV fluids
      • IV antihistamine
      • steroid against biphasic reaction
      • monitored bed / ICU ± vasopressors
      • glucagon if on beta blockers
      • allergy referral
      • education and adrenaline auto-injector
      8. Anaphylaxis — ibuprofen despite aspirin allergy (60M, knee OA)
      Task
      • ABC first
      • then angry patient framework
      Flushing DDx
      • emotional / menopause / autonomic
      • carcinoid
      • pheochromocytoma
      • mastocytosis
      • anaphylaxis
      • medullary thyroid cancer
      • serotonin syndrome
      • VIPoma
      • rosacea
      • drugs (CCB, vancomycin, steroids, niacin)
      • alcohol
      • spicy food
      Diagnosis
      • anaphylactic (distributive) shock
      • same management as case 7
      9. Biologic therapy counseling — RA on methotrexate (26F)
      Explore
      • disease duration and activity, extra-articular features
      • doses, adherence, reasons MTX failed (smoking, interactions, fibromyalgia, OSA)
      • comorbidities
      • infection review (recent infection, TB, shingles, HIV / hepatitis risk factors)
      • HF, lung disease, demyelination, thrombosis, malignancy
      • vaccines (flu, pneumococcal, zoster before starting)
      • pregnancy / breastfeeding
      Educate
      • side effects (allergy, infection, TB reactivation) with leaflet
      • dose, duration, technique
      • questions and consent
      Pre-biologic tests
      • CBC (with differential)
      • inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP)
      • renal profile (creatinine, eGFR)
      • liver profile
      • lipid profile (tocilizumab, JAKi)
      • urinalysis
      • infection screen (HBV — HBsAg, anti-HBc; HCV; HIV; latent TB — TST or QuantiFERON)
      • imaging (CXR)
      Approved
      • TNFi (infliximab, etanercept, adalimumab, certolizumab, golimumab)
      • IL-6 (tocilizumab, sarilumab)
      • rituximab
      • abatacept
      • JAKi (tofacitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib)
      10. Cyclophosphamide counseling — lupus nephritis class III with crescents (30F)
      Regimens
      • Euro-Lupus 500 mg IV every 2 weeks × 6
      • NIH 0.5–1 g/m² IV monthly × 6
      Side effects
      • infertility / premature ovarian failure (higher > 30 years)
      • marrow suppression and infection
      • hemorrhagic cystitis and bladder cancer
      • teratogenic
      • SIADH
      • hypotension
      • nausea
      • alopecia
      Risk reduction
      • GnRH agonist (leuprolide 3.75 mg IM 10–14 days before)
      • IV hydration
      • MESNA (25% of the dose before and after)
      • ondansetron + dexamethasone
      • TMP-SMX PCP prophylaxis 3× / week
      • contraception
      Stop and call
      • rash / allergy
      • infection
      • severe vomiting
      • jaundice
      • hematuria
      • planning pregnancy
      High dose preferred
      • nephritic sediment with GFR 25–80
      • or crescents / necrosis in > 25% of glomeruli
      Induction
      • CYC or MMF
      • **maintenance:** MMF or azathioprine
      • **activity / relapse antibody:** anti-C1q
      Questions asked across several stations
      Error disclosure essentials
      • acknowledge
      • apologize
      • explain what happened and what you are doing about it
      • incident report
      • transparent review
      • complaint route
      • continuity of care
      Warfarin reversal by INR
      • INR above range < 10, no bleeding → hold doses
      • INR > 10, no bleeding → hold + oral vitamin K 2.5–5 mg
      • major bleeding at any INR → IV vitamin K 5–10 mg + 4F-PCC (FFP if PCC unavailable)
      Cancer-associated VTE
      • LMWH or DOAC (avoid DOAC in GI / GU luminal cancers with bleeding risk)
      • continue while cancer is active

      Communication & Counseling

      How to use this

      Built from the two communication-skills files (Dr. Moayad Aljuhani's Arabic scripts, Family Medicine OSCE First Aid pages, KAMC-Jeddah examiner forms) and the counseling checklists (general counseling, inhaler technique, kidney biopsy, post-PCI care). The Qassim communication stations themselves (lung and colon cancer, angry patients, biologics, cyclophosphamide) are in the General and Miscellaneous tabs.

      Language
      • the counseling is done in Arabic in the exam — the key Arabic phrases are given with each framework
      Which framework
      • major bad news → introduction then SPIKES
      • procedure or consent (transfusion, dialysis, biopsy) → introduction then ESAR
      • new drug → medication framework
      • angry patient or error → angry-patient framework
      Common mistake
      • if a station starts as a history / exam case and turns into a communication task
      • introduce yourself again before the communication part
      Topics covered
      • angry patient
      • medical error disclosure
      • breaking bad news (cancer, HIV, hepatitis B)
      • hepatitis C counseling
      • pre-HIV-test counseling
      • blood transfusion consent
      • DNAR discussion
      • starting a medication (warfarin, steroids, methotrexate, metformin, statins)
      • inhaler technique
      • hemodialysis initiation
      • kidney biopsy consent
      • post-PCI clinic
      • smoking cessation
      • pre-pregnancy counseling (SLE, sickle cell, pulmonary hypertension)
      • pre-operative counseling
      1. Opening and closing every station
      Opening (English)
