      • introduce yourself and your role
      • confirm name and age / date of birth with two identifiers
      • explain the purpose
      • ask permission
      • private setting (phone and pager off, door closed)
      • ask if they want a relative present
      • sit down at eye level
      Opening (Arabic)
      • "السلام عليكم، أنا الدكتور/ة ____ من قسم الباطنة والفريق المعالج"
      • "ممكن تزودني باسمك وعمرك؟"
      • "هل الوقت مناسب لمناقشة حالتك الصحية؟"
      • "هل ترغب بوجود أحد أفراد عائلتك خلال النقاش؟"
      Ideas, concerns, expectations (ICE)
      • "ماذا تعرف عن حالتك الصحية؟"
      • "هل عندك أي مخاوف أو توقعات؟"
      Closing (English)
      • summarize
      • check understanding (teach-back)
      • invite questions
      • safety net (when to come to the ER)
      • contact number
      • leaflets / websites / support groups
      • follow-up appointment
      • thank the patient
      Closing (Arabic)
      • "هل جميع الأمور واضحة؟ أو تحتاج أعيد نقطة معينة؟"
      • "إذا حصل أي أمر طارئ تقدر تجي على الطوارئ مباشرة أو تتواصل مع العيادة على هذا الرقم"
      • "هذي منشورات ومواقع ومجموعات دعم ممكن تستفيد منها"
      • "شكراً جزيلاً لحضورك ووقتك"
      2. Frameworks
      SPIKES — breaking bad news
      Setting
      • private room
      • phones off
      • door closed
      • tissues and water
      • relative if wanted
      • sit down
      • enough time
      Perception
      • "What do you understand about why we did these tests?" — "هل عندك خلفية عن سبب التحاليل؟"
      Invitation
      • "Would you like me to go through the results in detail?" — "هل ترغب أن أبدأ بالنتيجة وأشرح التشخيص؟"
      Knowledge
      • warning shot ("I'm afraid the results are not what we hoped")
      • plain words, small chunks
      • no jargon ("weakens" not "decreased", "blood test" not "serology")
      • no bluntness
      • never "nothing more we can do"
      Emotions
      • allow silence, keep eye contact
      • observe → name the emotion → link it to the news → empathic statement ("I can see this is very hard for you")
      Strategy and summary
      • MDT plan (oncology, surgery, palliative, social worker, psychologist)
      • referral with a named specialist
      • follow-up soon
      • hope without false hope
      • offer help getting home
      ESAR — procedures and consent
      E — Explanation and knowledge
      • what they know
      • why it is needed (their condition)
      • what the procedure is in simple words
      • aim
      • duration
      S — Safety and risks
      • benefits
      • common and serious complications and how they are minimized
      A — Alternatives
      • other options and their limits
      • risks of refusing
      R — Reassurance and consent
      • questions
      • written consent (name, signature, date)
      • leaflets
      • follow-up
      • thank
      Starting a medication
      Explore
      • what they know
      • current drugs and allergies
      • contraindications (pregnancy, liver / kidney disease)
      • adherence barriers
      • hidden agenda
      Explain
      • why it is started
      • how it works in lay terms
      • how to take it
      • how long
      • baseline and monitoring tests
      Risks
      • common side effects
      • rare but serious ones
      • how to reduce them (e.g. alendronate with long-term steroids)
      • specific hazards (adrenal crisis if steroids stopped suddenly)
      Safety net
      • what to report and when to seek urgent help
      • carry a steroid card / warfarin booklet
      • store away from children
      Close
      • solutions to compliance problems (dosette box, alarms)
      • leaflet
      • follow-up
      Disease counseling
      Content
      • normal physiology → what the disease is → why it happens → complications → aims of treatment → lifestyle + medicines → red flags / when to seek help
      Angry patient and error disclosure
      Order
      • stabilize the patient first (ABC) if unwell → introduce → safety (door open, sit near the door, safe distance; say it aloud) → let them vent without interrupting → acknowledge → apologize → explain → actions → complaint route → follow-up → close
      Arabic
      • "ممكن تشرح لي سبب غضبك؟"
      • "أنا متفهم جداً لشعورك وقلقك"
      • "نعتذر لك على اللي حصل، وسنضع خطة لتخفيف الضرر الحاصل"
      • "سنرفع الأمر لرئيس القسم لتفادي حدوثه في المستقبل"
      • "نقدر نحولك للأخصائي الاجتماعي أو تجربة المريض لتقديم شكوى رسمية، وهذا حقك ولن يؤثر على سير علاجك"
      Never
      • argue
      • blame a colleague or department
      • be defensive
      3. Stations
      Angry patient and errors
      1. Angry patient (adverse event, e.g. drug allergy)
      Emotions
      • ask the reason
      • full attention, eye contact
      • no interruption
      • empathy
      Apology
      • apologize sincerely and accept the grievance
      • the aim is to treat the problem (e.g. infection needed an antibiotic)
      Actions
      • treat the current problem (e.g. antihistamines, document the allergy in the file)
      • report to the head of department and clinics
      • meeting with the treating team (involve nephrology etc. as needed)
      • social worker / patient-experience office for a formal complaint
      • follow-up appointment or phone call
      Close
      • emergency contact
      • leaflets
      • check understanding
      • questions
      • thank
      2. Medical error disclosure (e.g. pneumothorax after central line; wrong antibiotic dose → AKI)
      Approach
      • same as the angry patient
      • but you volunteer the error
      Explain honestly
      • what happened ("your condition needed an antibiotic, but I gave you an incorrect dose and this raised your kidney tests")
      • the harm
      • what is being done now
      Then
      • apology
      • plan to reduce harm
      • full reports and results
      • incident report and review to prevent recurrence
      • complaint route
      • follow-up
      Breaking bad news
      3. Breaking bad news — cancer
      Framework
      • SPIKES
      Knowledge (Arabic example)
      • "الفترة الماضية كنت تشكي من نقص في الوزن وفقدان شهية وعقدة في الغدة اللمفاوية، لذلك أخذنا خزعة للتأكد من التشخيص، وتبين وجود خلايا سرطانية" — in a calm voice
      • show empathy
      Strategy
      • "الفريق الطبي رح يرسم لك خطة علاجية متكاملة"
      • referral to a named oncologist
      • early appointment
      • social worker / psychologist
      4. Breaking bad news — HIV infection
      Before the result
      • rule out risk factors (multiple partners, tattoos, transfusion, surgery, IV drugs)
      • occupation, marital status
      • ask if they like details
      Disclosure
      • confirmed result only (repeat / confirmatory test)
      • "I'm sorry to tell you your results came back positive for HIV" → pause, tissues, allow emotions, offer rest
      Knowledge
      • a virus that weakens the immune system
      • spread by blood, sex, mother-to-child (pregnancy, delivery, breastfeeding), shared needles
      • treatment is available locally — not curative but controls the virus, near-normal life if taken regularly
      • earlier treatment = fewer complications
      Protecting others
      • condoms (water-based lubricants), no sharing needles or razors, no blood or organ donation, tell your dentist and surgeons
      • inform the wife — MOH has a clinic for testing spouses and children
      • inform previous sexual partners
      Plan
      • CD4, viral load, STI screen
      • ID referral
      • start ART immediately regardless of CD4 (START / TEMPRANO)
      • social worker / psychologist
      Vaccines at next visit
      • hepatitis B, influenza, pneumococcal, Td / Tdap for all
      • hepatitis A, MMR and varicella only if CD4 ≥ 200 cells/µL
      Children
      • with effective ART (undetectable viral load) transmission to partner and baby is near zero
      • assess the wife's status first
      Board questions
      • disclose only after confirmation
      • report to infection control and MOH
      • encourage the patient to tell his wife (hospital / MOH policy)
      • safety net — fever, rash, chest infection, weight loss
      5. Breaking bad news — hepatitis B
      Setting and disclosure
      • as for HIV
      • ask about jaundice, abdominal pain, vomiting, fatigue
      Knowledge
      • common worldwide, preventable by vaccine
      • affects the liver
      • can clear, become a carrier, or become chronic (> 6 months)
      Transmission
      • blood, saliva, genital secretions — shared razors, blades, toothbrushes, sex
      • not by hugging, sneezing, coughing, breastfeeding or sharing cups
      Daily life
      • no restrictions
      • balanced diet
      • avoid alcohol
      • test and vaccinate family and spouse
      • condoms
      • hepatitis A vaccine
      Complications
      • cirrhosis / liver failure (≈ 20% of chronic) and hepatocellular carcinoma (≈ 15%) if not managed
      • antivirals slow damage
      Plan
      • liver tests, STI screen, ultrasound, hepatology referral
      • safety net — jaundice, itching, bleeding, sleep disturbance
      6. Counseling a patient with hepatitis C
      Ask
      • ICE
      • since when, how acquired, treatment so far
      • symptoms (weight loss, jaundice, dark urine, pale stool, abdominal pain)
      • smoking, sexual activity, drugs
      Explain
      • infects the liver
      • spread by needles, razors, toothbrushes, multiple partners (rare through modern transfusion)
      • not by casual contact
      • 70–80% become chronic
      • cirrhosis in 15–30% after 20–30 years, then HCC
      Treatment (updated)
      • direct-acting antivirals for 8–12 weeks cure > 95% (the source page mentions interferon / lamivudine — outdated)
      Protect yourself
      • no alcohol
      • no drugs or herbs without advice
      • hepatitis A and B vaccines
      • safe sex
      Protect others
      • don't share toothbrushes / razors / needles
      • clean blood spills with bleach
      • cover wounds
      • no blood donation
      • test the spouse
      Refer
      • hepatology
      • addiction services if IV drug use / alcohol
      • safety net as for HBV
      7. Pre-test HIV counseling
      Explain the reason
      • risk assessment — STIs
      • partner HIV-positive
      • men who have sex with men
      • partner from a high-prevalence country
      • IV drug use
      • transfusion abroad
      • sex work
      Knowledge
      • HIV vs AIDS
      • most positive people are asymptomatic
      • transmission routes
      Benefits of testing
      • if positive — effective treatment, normal life expectancy, can have children, reduces transmission, counseling available
      • if negative — ends uncertainty
      The test
      • antigen / antibody blood test (4th generation)
      • window period ≈ 3 months → repeat at 3 months if recent exposure
      • point-of-care tests miss HIV-2
      Confidentiality
      • results kept confidential
      • consent
      • how and when results will be given
      Procedures and consent (ESAR)
      8. Blood transfusion consent
      Explanation
      • because of bleeding / severe anemia / marrow failure your body is not making enough blood
      • transfusion = giving donated blood through a cannula in the arm
      • aim = replace blood and prevent low BP or organ failure
      • each unit takes 1–4 hours
      Risks
      • acute hemolytic reaction
      • very rare viral infection (blood is screened for HIV, hepatitis B and C)
      • fever, rash, breathlessness
      • fluid overload in heart or kidney patients
      Alternatives
      • IV iron
      • erythropoiesis-stimulating agents — not as effective in an emergency
      Reassurance and consent
      • every unit is carefully cross-matched
      • questions
      • written consent (name, signature, date)
      • leaflet
      9. Hemodialysis initiation (e.g. 60M, diabetic CKD, eGFR 10, nausea, itching)
      Indications to explain
      • kidneys can no longer remove waste
      • extra fluid or balance salts — uremia
      • volume overload
      • acidosis
      • high potassium
      What it is
      • a machine acts as an "artificial kidney"
      • blood passes through tubing, is cleaned and returned
      • 3 sessions a week, 3–5 hours each
      Access
      • AV fistula (best, small operation joining artery to vein in the arm)
      • AV graft (synthetic tube if fistula fails)
      • central venous catheter in the neck or groin (temporary)
      Benefits
      • less fatigue, nausea and breathlessness
      • better appetite and alertness
      • lower mortality
      Risks
      • fatigue, low BP episodes, cramps, itching, dizziness, nausea, headache
      • access infection or thrombosis
      • (peritonitis and hernia with peritoneal dialysis)
      Alternatives
      • peritoneal dialysis at home (needs strict hygiene)
      • kidney transplant (best long-term option, depends on donor)
      • conservative / palliative care in very elderly or advanced illness
      Refusing
      • toxin and fluid build-up → uremic coma
      • heart or lung failure
      • sudden death
      Close
      • dialysis is not a cure but keeps life stable
      • involve family
      • patient's role in the decision
      • another appointment to reconsider
      • written consent
      • leaflet
      Same station — CKD complications
      • anemia (iron if ferritin < 200 ng/mL and TSAT < 20%, then ESA; exclude B12 / folate / GI loss)
      • hyperphosphatemia (diet, binders)
      • metabolic acidosis (bicarbonate)
      • volume (diuretics, BP control)
      • hold metformin / sitagliptin
      • plan transplant vs HD / PD and access early
      10. Kidney biopsy consent
      Explanation
      • a small needle takes a small piece of kidney under local anesthesia with ultrasound guidance
      • benefit = diagnosis that guides treatment
      • risk of not doing it = delayed diagnosis and wrong treatment
      Before
      • stop antiplatelets / anticoagulants for the right time
      • CBC and coagulation
      • fast 8 hours
      • BP controlled (< 140/90)
      Complications
      • bleeding (hematuria, hematoma — may need transfusion or embolization)
      • infection
      • pain
      After
      • lie flat at least 4 hours
      • monitor vitals and urine color / output
      • analgesia
      • repeat CBC after 4–6 hours
      • most go home the same day
      • avoid strenuous activity
      Alternatives
      • blood tests and imaging only (less precise)
      Red flags
      • visible blood in urine
      • confusion
      • dizziness / low BP
      • severe pain
      • fever
      • low urine output or inability to pass urine
      Close
      • clinic follow-up for the result
      • questions
      • consent
      • thank
      11. DNAR discussion (with patient or family)
      Setting
      • private, phone off, door closed
      • family present if wanted
      • explore what they understand
      Explain the situation
      • the patient is critically ill in ICU and receiving all recommended treatment, but because of severity, chronic illnesses and age, the chance of responding is very low
      • three consultants met and agreed that CPR would not be in the patient's best interest if the heart stops
      What DNAR means
      • no CPR if the heart stops
      • all other treatment continues (medicines, oxygen, feeding, fluids, comfort)
      • "not withdrawing care — avoiding a futile intervention" — "القرار لا يعني سحب العلاج بل منع تدخل غير مجدٍ"
      Review
      • reversed if the patient improves
      • part of advance care planning
      • consultant signs the form
      • inform the MDT
      Emotions
      • let them speak without interruption
      • "أتفهم تماماً صعوبة هذا القرار... نحن معكم ونحترم مشاعركم"
      Close
      • social worker
      • offer to explain to other family members
      • contact number
      • leaflet
      Medication counseling
      12. Starting warfarin (AF with mitral stenosis)
      Explain
      • palpitations led to tests showing AF with a narrowed mitral valve → blood thinner warfarin, tablet once daily
      • reduces stroke risk by about 70%
      • LMWH injections until INR is therapeutic
      Monitoring
      • INR blood tests (target 2–3; every 8–12 weeks once stable)
      Side effects
      • bleeding, bruising, rash, abdominal pain
      • teratogenic
      Warnings
      • keep vitamin K foods steady (leafy greens, green tea)
      • interactions — antibiotics, herbs, multivitamins, NSAIDs
      • tell the dentist
      • careful shaving
      • not safe in pregnancy — plan with your doctor
      • head injury → urgent review
      • wear a warfarin bracelet / carry the booklet
      Alternatives
      • DOACs — not suitable with moderate–severe mitral stenosis or mechanical valves
      13. Common drugs — summary table
      Prednisolone
      • stops the immune system attacking itself / reduces inflammation
      • not a painkiller
      • side effects — ulcer, osteoporosis, weight gain, Cushingoid, diabetes, HTN, infection, cataract
      • hazard — adrenal crisis if stopped suddenly
      • carry a steroid card, increase dose in illness (sick-day rules)
      • monitor glucose, BP, DEXA
      • give alendronate for long courses
      Warfarin
      • thins the blood to prevent clots
      • bleeding, bruising, rash, alopecia, teratogenic
      • interactions with alcohol, antibiotics, antiepileptics
      • INR monitoring
      • report bleeding or any head injury
      • bracelet
      Methotrexate
      • once weekly — never daily
      • GI upset, mouth ulcers, infections, rash, hair thinning, marrow suppression, liver fibrosis, pneumonitis, teratogenic (men and women)
      • baseline CBC, renal, LFT, CXR
      • folic acid
      • avoid alcohol
      • no live vaccines
      • report breathlessness, jaundice, fever, sore throat, bruising
      Metformin
      • makes the body more sensitive to insulin
      • with meals
      • GI upset
      • no hypoglycemia alone
      • risk of lactic acidosis in kidney failure or hypoxia
      • hold before contrast and surgery
      • monitor renal function and HbA1c
      Statin
      • lowers cholesterol and cardiovascular risk
      • muscle aches, liver injury
      • report muscle pain, weakness, dark urine or jaundice
      • lipid profile and LFT
      Biologics (RA / SpA)
      • see General tab — infection and TB screen
      • vaccines
      • injection technique
      14. Inhaler technique (asthma — salbutamol + beclomethasone)
      Explain asthma
      • narrowing of the airways from inflammation / allergy
      • triggers (cold air, exercise, dust, pollen, smoke)
      Reliever vs preventer
      • blue salbutamol = reliever when symptoms (2 puffs; side effects tremor, palpitations, headache)
      • brown beclomethasone = preventer, every day even without symptoms (sore throat / oral thrush — rinse mouth)
      MDI steps
      • check expiry and dose counter → remove cap
      • check mouthpiece → shake well → sit upright
      • chin slightly up → breathe out fully away from the inhaler → tight seal with lips → press once while breathing in slowly and deeply → hold breath 10 seconds (or as long as comfortable) → breathe out slowly → wait 30–60 seconds before a second puff → replace cap → rinse mouth after steroid
      Teach-back
      • patient demonstrates
      • give feedback
      • explain spacer use if needed
      When to seek help
      • no improvement after 4 puffs of the blue inhaler (UK guide: 10 puffs, 1 every 30–60 s, then call emergency)
      • cannot speak full sentences
      • blue lips
      • drowsy
      Arabic steps
      • "رج البخاخ جيداً، أزل الغطاء، أخرج النفس تماماً، ضع فمك حول الفوهة بإحكام، اضغط مرة واحدة أثناء الشهيق ببطء وعمق، احبس النفس 10 ثواني، أخرج النفس ببطء، وبعد بخاخ الكورتيزون تمضمض"
      Close
      • leaflet
      • follow-up to recheck technique
      • school nurse can hold inhalers
      • questions
      Clinics and lifestyle
      15. First clinic after PCI
      History
      • chest pain and all cardiac symptoms
      • access-site pain or bleeding (radial / femoral)
      • low mood
      • when PCI was done, number and type of stents, complications
      • medications, doses, adherence, side effects
      • home BP, comorbidities, smoking, diet
      • return to exercise, sex and driving
      Exam
      • HR and BP (to titrate drugs)
      • heart and lungs
      • leg edema
      • access site for hematoma, pseudoaneurysm, AV fistula
      Review
      • cath report
      • echo (if EF low, repeat after ≥ 90 days of optimal therapy — ICD decision)
      • creatinine, potassium, LDL, HbA1c, ECG
      Adjust
      • DAPT duration by stent type and ACS vs elective
      • beta blocker to HR 50–60
      • high-intensity statin, add ezetimibe if LDL > 1.4 mmol/L (55 mg/dL)
      • HF drugs guided by creatinine, K and BP
      Lifestyle
      • driving after 1–2 weeks if uncomplicated (4 weeks after cardiogenic shock)
      • work and air travel after about 2 weeks
      • sex after 4–6 weeks (sooner if low risk)
      • daily activities immediately
      • cardiac rehab
      • stop smoking
      • vaccines
      • weight loss
      • treat DM and HTN
      16. Smoking cessation
      History
      • age started
      • cigarettes per day / pack-years
      • type (cigarette, shisha)
      • previous quit attempts, how long, why relapsed
      • why quit now
      • symptoms (cough, dyspnea, weight loss, night sweats)
      • support at home
      • depression, epilepsy (bupropion), alcohol / drugs
      Advise
      • stopping is the single best thing for your health
      • link to their symptoms
      • benefits (heart, lungs, fertility, money, appearance)
      Assess readiness
      • stage of change
      • if ready → plan
      • if unsure → explore barriers gently
      Plan
      • quit date within 2 weeks
      • smoking diary and gradual reduction
      • avoid triggers
      • tell family and friends
      • exercise when craving
      • withdrawal symptoms (irritability, appetite, headache, insomnia) are temporary
      Drugs
      • nicotine replacement (gum 4 mg every 1–2 h, max 20/day, patches, inhaler; 8–12 weeks; mouth irritation, insomnia)
      • bupropion 150 mg daily × 3 days then twice daily for 7–12 weeks, start 1 week before quit date (insomnia, dry mouth, headache, seizures — avoid in epilepsy)
      • varenicline 0.5 mg → 1 mg twice daily for 12 weeks, start 1 week before (nausea, insomnia, vivid dreams)
      Follow-up
      • within 2–4 weeks
      • smoking-cessation clinic
      • screen for cardiovascular risk
      17. Pre-pregnancy counseling
      SLE
      • plan pregnancy only after ≥ 6 months of inactive disease
      • stop methotrexate, mycophenolate, cyclophosphamide (switch in advance)
      • continue hydroxychloroquine, azathioprine, low-dose steroids
      • folic acid
      • screen aPL, renal function, BP, urine protein, anti-Ro / La
      • risks — miscarriage, preeclampsia, preterm birth, IUGR, flare, neonatal lupus
      • joint rheumatology–obstetric care
      • low-dose aspirin
      Sickle cell disease
      • genetic counseling and test the partner
      • vaccines (pneumococcal, influenza, hepatitis B)
      • folic acid 5 mg daily
      • stop hydroxyurea (3 months before), ACEi / ARBs, iron chelators
      • baseline Hb, renal, liver, ferritin, echo if PH suspected
      • risks — miscarriage, preeclampsia, IUGR, crises, VTE, acute chest syndrome
      • avoid dehydration, cold, overexertion
      Pulmonary hypertension
      • strongly advise against pregnancy (very high maternal mortality, up to 30–50%)
      • reliable contraception — avoid estrogen, use progestin-only / IUD (bosentan reduces hormonal contraceptive efficacy)
      • if pregnant → specialist cardiology + obstetric care, discuss early termination
      • psychosocial support
      18. Pre-operative counseling
      Functional capacity
      • can climb 1–2 flights of stairs or walk briskly = ≥ 4 METs (good)
      • < 4 METs = higher risk, needs evaluation
      RCRI (1 point each)
      • IHD
      • HF
      • stroke / TIA
      • insulin-treated DM
      • creatinine > 2 mg/dL (177 µmol/L)
      • high-risk surgery → 0 = low (< 1%), 1–2 = intermediate (1–6%), ≥ 3 = high (> 6%)
      Explain simply
      • "your heart risk is low / moderate / high
      • we will take precautions"
      • risk ≠ cancellation
      Pre-op
      • CBC, electrolytes, renal and liver function, ECG, CXR if indicated
      • optimize BP, DM, COPD, CKD
      • continue beta blockers and statins
      • hold ACEi / ARB on the morning of surgery if advised
      • plan anticoagulants and antiplatelets
      • stop smoking ≥ 4 weeks before
      Peri-op
      • monitoring before, during and after
      • cardiology / anesthesia input
      • shared decision-making
      Questions asked across several stations
      Examiner global rating
      • organized approach
      • mixed open and closed questions
      • active listening
      • no jargon
      • responds to ICE
      • checks understanding
      • time management
      Who to involve
      • social worker
      • psychologist
      • patient-experience office (complaints)
      • named specialist
      • family (with consent)
      Safety net phrase
      • "If anything worries you — fever
      • bleeding
      • breathlessness
      • confusion — come to the ER directly or call the clinic on this number."

      Data Interpretation

      How to use this

      Short data-interpretation stations from the KFSHRC residency sets (PFT, peripheral smear, urinalysis). Each specialty tab also has its own "Data interpretation" section at the end with the images from its stations (ECGs, X-rays, CTs, smears, skin photos).

      Approach to any data station
      • describe what you see first (pattern, key abnormal values with units)
      • then give the diagnosis
      • then 2–3 differentials
      • then the next test and management
      Sets covered
      • PFT (obstructive, restrictive with normal DLCO)
      • peripheral smear (schistocytes, teardrop cells)
      • urinalysis (WBC cast, calcium oxalate, WBCs, squamous cells, uric acid, eosinophiluria)
      1. Pulmonary function tests
      How to read a PFT
      Step 1 — spirometry
      • FEV1/FVC < 0.70 (or < LLN; rough LLN = predicted − 8) = obstruction
      • FEV1/FVC normal or high with low FVC = possible restriction
      Step 2 — reversibility
      • post-bronchodilator FEV1 or FVC rise ≥ 12% and ≥ 200 mL = significant (asthma)
      Step 3 — lung volumes
      • TLC < 80% predicted confirms restriction
      • RV / TLC high = air trapping
      Step 4 — DLCO
      • low with restriction = parenchymal (ILD)
      • normal with restriction = extraparenchymal (chest wall, pleura, neuromuscular, obesity)
      • low with obstruction = emphysema
      • isolated low DLCO = pulmonary vascular disease or anemia
      • high DLCO = hemorrhage, polycythemia, asthma
      Severity (FEV1 % predicted)
      • mild ≥ 70%
      • moderate 60–69%
      • moderately severe 50–59%
      • severe 35–49%
      • very severe < 35%
      Case 1 — obstruction with marked reversibility (54M non-smoker, dyspnea and cough)
      Data
      • FVC 3.19 L (76%) → 4.00 L after BD (+25%)
      • FEV1 2.18 L (64%) → 2.83 L after BD (+30%, +650 mL)
      • FEV1/FVC 68% (LLN ≈ 72) → 71%
      Flow-volume loop
      • scooped (concave) expiratory limb below the predicted points
      • the post-bronchodilator loop is larger
      Pattern
      • FVC and FEV1 below 80% with FEV1/FVC 0.68 = airflow obstruction
      Severity
      • FEV1 64% = moderate obstruction
      Reversibility
      • +30% / +650 mL = significant (≥ 12% and ≥ 200 mL) → favors asthma
      Differential
      • asthma
      • COPD
      • bronchiectasis
      Case 2 — restriction with normal DLCO (36F non-smoker, exertional dyspnea)
      Data
      • FVC 0.88 L (26%)
      • FEV1 0.87 L (30%)
      • FEV1/FVC 99% (predicted 86)
      • RV 1.61 L (115%)
      • TLC 1.61 L (53%)
      • RV / TLC 65% (predicted 29)
      • DLCO 20 (75% — normal)
      Pattern
      • low FEV1 and FVC with preserved ratio
      • TLC 53% = moderate restrictive defect
      DLCO
      • normal → alveolar-capillary interface intact → extraparenchymal cause
      • high RV / TLC suggests poor expiratory muscle effort
      Differential
      • chest wall disorders (kyphoscoliosis, obesity)
      • pleural disease
      • neuromuscular disease (myasthenia, ALS, diaphragm palsy, myopathy)
      Next
      • MIP / MEP
      • supine vs upright FVC (diaphragm)
      • CK
      • AChR antibodies
      • chest imaging
      2. Peripheral blood smear
      Case 1 — schistocytes (33F, fever and bruising)
      Findings
      • schistocytes (helmet and triangular fragmented red cells)
      • few platelets
      Diagnosis
      • microangiopathic hemolytic anemia → TTP / HUS
      Investigations
      • ADAMTS13 activity
      • hemolysis panel (LDH, haptoglobin, indirect bilirubin, retics, DAT)
      • CBC with differential
      • creatinine
      • liver profile
      • coagulation (normal in TTP, abnormal in DIC)
      Management
      • urgent plasma exchange
      • glucocorticoids
      • rituximab
      • caplacizumab
      Case 2 — teardrop cells (44M, fatigue, massive splenomegaly)
      Data
      • Hb 9 g/dL (90 g/L), normocytic normochromic
      • WBC 4 ×10⁹/L
      • platelets 100 ×10⁹/L
      • early satiety
      Findings
      • teardrop cells (dacrocytes)
      • may come with a leukoerythroblastic picture (nucleated red cells + immature myeloid cells)
      Diagnosis
      • primary myelofibrosis (marrow fibrosis → extramedullary hematopoiesis)
      Next
      • JAK2 / CALR / MPL mutations
      • bone marrow biopsy (dry tap, reticulin fibrosis)
      Complications
      • worsening anemia and thrombocytopenia
      • massive splenomegaly, splenic infarction
      • portal hypertension
      • pulmonary hypertension
      • pleural effusion, ascites (extramedullary hematopoiesis)
      • liver failure
      • bone pain
      • marrow failure
      • transformation to acute leukemia
      3. Urinalysis — urine microscopy
      Image 1 — WBC cast
      Findings
      • cylindrical cast packed with white cells (lobulated nuclei)
      Meaning
      • renal parenchymal inflammation → pyelonephritis, acute interstitial nephritis
      • also proliferative GN
      Image 2 — calcium oxalate crystals
      Findings
      • envelope-shaped (bipyramidal) dihydrate crystals (arrowhead) and dumbbell / oval monohydrate crystals (arrow)
      Meaning
      • most common stone type
      • monohydrate needles / dumbbells in large numbers → ethylene glycol poisoning (with high anion and osmolar gap)
      • also hyperoxaluria (enteric — Crohn's, gastric bypass)
      Image 3 — white blood cells
      Findings
      • many round granular cells with lobulated nuclei
      • larger than RBCs
      Meaning
      • pyuria → UTI
      • sterile pyuria → TB, interstitial nephritis, partially treated UTI, stones, chlamydia
      Image 4 — squamous epithelial cells
      Findings
      • large flat polygonal cells with small central nuclei
      Meaning
      • contamination from the urethra / vagina → poorly collected sample
      • repeat a clean-catch midstream specimen
      Image 5 — uric acid crystals
      Findings
      • rhomboid / plate-like crystals
      • birefringent (multicolored) under polarized light (inset)
      Meaning
      • acidic urine
      • gout, tumor lysis syndrome (acute uric acid nephropathy), uric acid stones (radiolucent)
      Image 6 — eosinophiluria (Hansel stain)
      Findings
      • cells with bilobed nuclei and bright red-orange granules on Hansel stain
      Meaning
      • acute interstitial nephritis (drugs — β-lactams, PPIs, NSAIDs)
      • also atheroembolic disease, pyelonephritis, prostatitis (low specificity)
      Questions asked across several stations
      Casts
      • hyaline = normal / concentrated urine
      • RBC = glomerulonephritis
      • WBC = pyelonephritis / AIN
      • muddy brown granular = ATN
      • waxy / broad = advanced CKD
      • fatty (oval fat bodies, Maltese cross) = nephrotic syndrome
      Crystals
      • calcium oxalate (envelope / dumbbell)
      • uric acid (rhomboid, acid urine)
      • struvite (coffin lid, Proteus, alkaline urine)
      • cystine (hexagonal, cystinuria)
      • calcium phosphate (alkaline urine)
      Smear clues
      • schistocytes = MAHA
      • spherocytes = AIHA / hereditary spherocytosis
      • bite / blister cells = G6PD
      • teardrop = myelofibrosis
      • target cells = thalassemia, liver disease
      • sickle cells = SCD
      • smudge cells = CLL
      • Auer rods = AML
      • hypersegmented neutrophils = B12 / folate deficiency
